A continuous audio stream is first converted by a short-time Fourier transform into a 2D time-frequency spectrogram (or Mel-frequency cepstral coefficients), so 2D convolutional filters can slide across time and frequency bins to detect acoustic phoneme signatures. Models that listen for “Hey Siri” or “Ok Google” run on always-on microcontrollers with kilobyte-scale memory and milliwatt power budgets (Warden and Situnayake 2020; C. R. Banbury et al. 2021; C. Banbury et al. 2021), so their designers count every byte and cycle.

Warden, Pete. 2018. “Speech Commands: A Dataset for Limited-Vocabulary Speech Recognition.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1804.03209, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.1804.03209.
Warden, Pete, and Daniel Situnayake. 2020. TinyML: Machine Learning with TensorFlow Lite on Arduino and Ultra-Low-Power Microcontrollers. O’Reilly Media.
Banbury, Colby R, Chuteng Zhou, Igor Fedorov, Ramon Matas, Urmish Thakker, Dibakar Gope, Vijay Janapa Reddi, Matthew Mattina, and Paul N Whatmough. 2021. “MicroNets: Neural Network Architectures for Deploying TinyML Applications on Commodity Microcontrollers.” Proceedings of Machine Learning and Systems 3: 517–32.
Banbury, Colby, Vijay Janapa Reddi, Peter Torelli, Jeremy Holleman, Nat Jeffries, Csaba Kiraly, Pietro Montino, et al. 2021. “MLPerf Tiny Benchmark.” arXiv Preprint.

Lighthouse 6.3: KWS (TinyML lighthouse)
Why it matters: Keyword spotting models (like DS-CNN) represent the power-constrained end of the spectrum. Used in always-on applications like smart doorbells that wake a larger vision pipeline only after an audio trigger, these models must run on microcontrollers with milliwatt power budgets.

At 200K parameters, a DS-CNN keyword spotter’s 800 KB of FP32 weights already exceeds the few hundred kilobytes of memory a typical microcontroller can devote to a model, so the model fits only after 8-bit quantization cuts it fourfold. KWS is therefore the lighthouse for extreme quantization (INT8/INT4) and specialized architectural primitives (depthwise separable convolutions) that trade theoretical representational power for maximum efficiency per watt.

From ResNet-50’s compute-heavy standard convolutions through MobileNet’s depthwise separable variants to KWS’s micro-power deployment, CNNs demonstrate how architectural constraints transform data structure into hardware efficiency. Local connectivity and weight sharing reduce parameter storage and expose reuse. Depth is what lets those stacks compose edges into objects, and ResNet-50’s fifty layers depend on two building blocks that make depth trainable at all.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A \(3 \times 3\) convolutional layer with 64 input channels and 64 output channels processes a \(224 \times 224\) feature map, producing 64 spatial output channels. How does its parameter count compare with a dense layer that maps the flattened \(224 \times 224 \times 64\) input to 64 scalar outputs, and why are the outputs not shape-equivalent?

    1. The CNN requires \(205\text{ million}\) parameters, whereas the dense layer requires only \(36{,}864\) parameters due to flattened matrix vectorization.
    2. The CNN requires \(3 \times 3 \times 64 \times 64 = 36{,}864\) parameters (~37K), whereas a dense layer mapping a \(224^2 \times 64\) input to 64 scalar outputs requires \(224^2 \times 64 \times 64 \approx 205\text{ million}\) parameters; the output shapes are not equivalent.
    3. Both architectures require exactly the same number of parameters because both perform 64-to-64 channel transformations.
    4. The CNN requires 9 parameters because spatial weight sharing reduces all kernel weights across all channels to a single \(3 \times 3\) matrix.

  2. Distinguish between translation equivariance (\(f(\mathcal{T}(\mathbf{x})) = \mathcal{T}(f(\mathbf{x}))\)) and translation invariance (\(f(\mathcal{T}(\mathbf{x})) = f(\mathbf{x})\)). Explain why intermediate convolutional layers must maintain equivariance for object detection while final classification layers often apply global average pooling to achieve invariance.

  3. Order the sequence of operations performed when executing a 2D convolution layer via the standard im2col lowering transformation followed by activation:

  1. Multiply the unfolded patch matrix by the stacked filter weight matrix using a standard GEMM library call
  2. Reshape and fold the resulting 2D GEMM output matrix back into the 4D spatial feature map tensor \((B, C_{\text{out}}, H_{\text{out}}, W_{\text{out}})\)
  3. Unfold overlapping \(K \times K\) receptive field input patches into columns (or rows) of a 2D matrix
  4. Add channel bias vectors and apply the element-wise nonlinear activation function (e.g., ReLU)
  5. Receive the 4D input activation tensor of shape \((B, C_{\text{in}}, H_{\text{in}}, W_{\text{in}})\)

  1. Because MobileNetV2 requires roughly 14\(\times\) fewer FLOPs than ResNet-50 per \(224 \times 224\) image, it is guaranteed to execute at least 10\(\times\) faster on any data center GPU.

  2. A depthwise separable convolution decomposes standard convolution into two sequential operations: a ____ convolution that applies spatial filters to each input channel independently, followed by a \(1 \times 1\) pointwise convolution that projects and mixes channels across the depth dimension.

  3. In a deep CNN using stacked \(3 \times 3\) convolutional filters with stride 1 and padding, by how much does the receptive field side length increase with each additional layer, and what is the architectural implication for detecting large objects?

    1. Receptive field increases by 9 pixels per layer, allowing a 3-layer network to cover an entire \(224 \times 224\) image.
    2. Receptive field side length doubles with each layer, scaling exponentially as \(3^L\).
    3. Receptive field side length grows linearly by 2 pixels per layer (a 3-layer stack sees a \(7 \times 7\) region), requiring deep stacks of layers or downsampling (pooling/striding) to detect objects spanning \(100+\) pixels in high-resolution images.
    4. Receptive field remains strictly fixed at \(3 \times 3\) across all layers because convolutional filter weights are shared across positions.

See Answers →

6.4 Skip Connections and Normalization

When plain convolutional networks are stacked beyond roughly 20 layers, adding more layers can make training error worse rather than better: gradients vanish or explode on the way back through the stack, and the scale of intermediate activations drifts on the way forward. Convolution made deep vision networks affordable in parameter count, and two building blocks made them trainable. Skip connections keep gradients alive through dozens of layers, and normalization keeps the activations those layers produce within a stable numerical range. Both arrived with deep CNNs, both carry their own memory and bandwidth costs, and both reappear in the deep families that follow, most visibly the transformer.

6.4.1 Skip connections and gradient flow

Why gradients vanish or explode across depth, and why additive identity shortcuts (skip connections) resolve that barrier, follows directly from the chain rule, whose layer-by-layer Jacobian form appears in Error signal propagation. For a deep network with layers \(\LayerFunction_1, \LayerFunction_2, \ldots, \FinalLayerFunction\), the gradient of the loss \(\LossFunction\) with respect to the weights in layer 1 is: \[ \frac{\partial \LossFunction}{\partial \WeightMatrix^{(1)}} = \frac{\partial \LossFunction}{\partial \ActivationVector^{(\LayerCount)}} \cdot \frac{\partial \ActivationVector^{(\LayerCount)}}{\partial \PreactivationVector^{(\LayerCount)}} \cdot \frac{\partial \PreactivationVector^{(\LayerCount)}}{\partial \ActivationVector^{(\LayerCount-1)}} \cdot \ldots \cdot \frac{\partial \PreactivationVector^{(2)}}{\partial \ActivationVector^{(1)}} \cdot \frac{\partial \ActivationVector^{(1)}}{\partial \PreactivationVector^{(1)}} \cdot \frac{\partial \PreactivationVector^{(1)}}{\partial \WeightMatrix^{(1)}} \] where \(\PreactivationVector^{(\LayerIndex)}\) represents the preactivation and \(\ActivationVector^{(\LayerIndex)} = \SigmoidFunction(\PreactivationVector^{(\LayerIndex)})\) the postactivation output of layer \(\LayerIndex\). Each layer contributes both a weight transformation and an activation derivative \(\SigmoidFunction'(\PreactivationVector^{(\LayerIndex)})\). With sigmoid activations, whose derivative peaks at 0.25 (Sigmoid), the activation-derivative factors alone contribute at most \((0.25)^{\LayerCount}\) along a path. The full gradient also depends on the intervening weight matrices; when their transformations do not offset this attenuation, early layers receive negligible updates. Exploding gradients are the destabilizing counterpart. If each layer Jacobian expands a shared direction by about 1.5, that component grows exponentially, producing overflow, NaN (not-a-number) values, and training divergence rather than a silent stall.

ReLU removes the activation’s share of this decay, since its derivative is one on active paths (ReLU), and that change made networks of 10–20 layers trainable. It does not remove the problem. Inactive units pass zero gradient, and a poorly initialized weight or a large update can leave a unit permanently dead. More fundamentally, ReLU does nothing to regulate the singular values of the weight matrices, which continue to attenuate or amplify gradients across depth. The original ResNet study showed the consequence on CIFAR-10: a 56-layer plain network had higher training and test error than a 20-layer plain network throughout training (He et al. 2016a). Because the deeper network was worse on the training set, the failure was an optimization barrier, not overfitting.

A residual block gives the gradient a direct path back through its input. Equation 6 adds the identity path \(\LayerInputVector\) to the residual mapping \(\ResidualFunction(\LayerInputVector)\): \[ \ResidualBlockOutput= \ResidualFunction(\LayerInputVector) + \LayerInputVector \tag{6}\] where \(\ResidualFunction(\LayerInputVector)\) represents the residual function (typically two convolutional layers with batch normalization and ReLU) and \(\LayerInputVector\) is the identity skip connection. The original post-activation ResNet block (figure 7) routes an identity shortcut around two weight layers and the ReLU between them, adds it back at a junction, and then applies the final activation.

During backpropagation, the gradient flows through this addition, and applying the chain rule gives: \[ \frac{\partial \LossFunction}{\partial \LayerInputVector} = \frac{\partial \LossFunction}{\partial \ResidualBlockOutput} \cdot \left(\frac{\partial \ResidualFunction(\LayerInputVector)}{\partial \LayerInputVector} + \IdentityMatrix\right) = \frac{\partial \LossFunction}{\partial \ResidualBlockOutput} \cdot \ResidualFunction'(\LayerInputVector) + \frac{\partial \LossFunction}{\partial \ResidualBlockOutput} \]

Here \(\IdentityMatrix\) is the identity matrix, and the gradient decomposes into two additive terms. Even if the residual term \(\frac{\partial \LossFunction}{\partial \ResidualBlockOutput}\ResidualFunction'(\LayerInputVector)\) attenuates, the identity term passes \(\frac{\partial \LossFunction}{\partial \ResidualBlockOutput}\) directly to the earlier layer. While the residual branch can partially interfere with the identity signal, keeping \(\|\ResidualFunction'(\LayerInputVector)\|\) small keeps the block Jacobian close to identity and bounds its condition number. Through \(\LayerCount\) residual blocks, the gradient becomes: \[ \frac{\partial \LossFunction}{\partial \LayerInputVector^{(0)}} = \frac{\partial \LossFunction}{\partial \LayerInputVector^{(\LayerCount)}} \cdot \prod_{\LayerIndex=1}^{\LayerCount} \left(\ResidualFunction'_\LayerIndex(\LayerInputVector^{(\LayerIndex-1)}) + \IdentityMatrix\right) \]

Each factor \((\ResidualFunction'_\ell + \IdentityMatrix)\) contains the identity term, keeping the factor near identity when the residual-branch Jacobian is small. A plain network instead multiplies arbitrary layer Jacobians, and if their average gain is even slightly below one the product vanishes exponentially (\(0.9^{50} \approx 0.005\)). ResNets multiply near-identity factors. The deviations can still compound, and the additive path does not guarantee exact unit gain across arbitrary depth, but this structure improves conditioning enough to make networks with 100+ layers trainable. The CIFAR-10 experiments confirm it: a 56-layer residual network reached about 7.0 percent test error against 8.8 percent for its 20-layer residual counterpart, reversing the degradation seen in plain networks (He et al. 2016a). There is no single depth at which skip connections suddenly become mandatory; trainability also depends on initialization, normalization, optimizer dynamics, and block architecture (He et al. 2016b).

He, Kaiming, Xiangyu Zhang, Shaoqing Ren, and Jian Sun. 2016a. “Deep Residual Learning for Image Recognition.” 2016 IEEE Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (CVPR), 770–78. https://doi.org/10.1109/cvpr.2016.90.
He, Kaiming, Xiangyu Zhang, Shaoqing Ren, and Jian Sun. 2016b. “Identity Mappings in Deep Residual Networks.” Computer Vision – ECCV 2016, 630–45. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-46493-0_38.

The shortcuts carry a concrete hardware cost that every deep network pays. Evaluating \(\ResidualBlockOutput= \ResidualFunction(\LayerInputVector) + \LayerInputVector\) requires the input tensor \(\LayerInputVector\) to remain resident in memory throughout the execution of every operation in the branch \(\ResidualFunction(\LayerInputVector)\). This extended liveness window increases peak activation memory in accelerator HBM during the forward pass and preserves \(\LayerInputVector\) for backpropagation unless activation checkpointing is used (Activation checkpointing). The elementwise addition is also memory-bandwidth bound. It performs one addition per two loaded operands and one stored result, about 0.08 FLOP/byte in FP32 (one FLOP per 12 bytes), so un-fused residual merges become memory stalls.

Residual connections stabilize the backward gradient path, but they do not constrain the forward scale of intermediate activations. Unbounded variance across depth can still destabilize layer inputs, which calls for a complementary block: normalization.

6.4.2 Normalization and activation stability

Without normalization, small weight updates in early layers compound across depth, shifting and amplifying intermediate activations until later nonlinearities saturate or overflow and training requires tiny learning rates. Skip connections protect the backward pass, and normalization steadies the forward pass by re-centering and re-scaling those intermediate activations at each block. Batch normalization in CNNs17 (Ioffe and Szegedy 2015), layer normalization for sequence models, and RMSNorm18 for large language models illustrate different choices of reduction axes and statistics.

17 Batch normalization (BatchNorm): The original normalization layer (Ioffe and Szegedy 2015), which re-scales activations using per-mini-batch statistics. In one ImageNet experiment, it reached the target accuracy with 14\(\times\) fewer training steps. Its batch-size dependency and training-serving skew (switching from batch statistics to running averages at inference) are systems limitations that motivated alternatives: LayerNorm removed batch dependency for transformers, and RMSNorm removed mean centering.

Ioffe, Sergey, and Christian Szegedy. 2015. “Batch Normalization: Accelerating Deep Network Training by Reducing Internal Covariate Shift.” Proceedings of the 32nd International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML) 37: 448–56.

18 RMSNorm (root mean square normalization): Introduced by Zhang and Sennrich (2019) at NeurIPS, RMSNorm simplifies LayerNorm by normalizing with the root mean square alone, dropping the mean-centering step. Across the paper’s tested models and implementations, RMSNorm reduced overall running time by 7–64 percent relative to LayerNorm. LLaMA-family and Mixtral-style transformer reports use RMSNorm (Touvron, Lavril, et al. 2023; Touvron, Martin, et al. 2023; Jiang et al. 2024), illustrating why a simpler normalization kernel matters at large language model scale.

Zhang, Biao, and Rico Sennrich. 2019. “Root Mean Square Layer Normalization.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS).
Touvron, Hugo, Thibaut Lavril, Gautier Izacard, Xavier Martinet, Marie-Anne Lachaux, Timothée Lacroix, Baptiste Rozière, et al. 2023. “LLaMA: Open and Efficient Foundation Language Models.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2302.13971.
Touvron, Hugo, Louis Martin, Kevin Stone, Peter Albert, Amjad Almahairi, Yasmine Babaei, Nikolay Bashlykov, et al. 2023. “Llama 2: Open Foundation and Fine-Tuned Chat Models.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2307.09288.
Jiang, A. Q., A. Sablayrolles, A. Roux, A. Mensch, B. Savary, C. Bamford, D. S. Chaplot, et al. 2024. “Mixtral of Experts.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2401.04088.

Batch normalization standardizes activations using statistics computed across the mini-batch for each feature or channel during training. For fully connected activations, the reduction axis is the batch dimension. For convolutional activations, implementations compute per-channel statistics over both the batch and spatial positions (\(\BatchSize \times \FeatureMapHeight\times \FeatureMapWidth\)). For a mini-batch \(\MiniBatchSet= \{\MiniBatchActivation_1, \ldots, \FinalMiniBatchActivation\}\) of activations at a particular layer, the transformation proceeds in two stages, starting with the mini-batch mean and variance: \[ \BatchNormMean= \frac{1}{\BatchSize}\sum_{i=1}^{\BatchSize} \MiniBatchActivation_i \qquad \BatchNormStandardDeviation^2 = \frac{1}{\BatchSize}\sum_{i=1}^{\BatchSize} (\MiniBatchActivation_i - \BatchNormMean)^2 \]

The second stage normalizes each activation over the batch and applies a learned scale and shift. The normalization step in equation 7 centers and scales activations, while equation 8 lets the network learn a useful output scale and offset: \[ \NormalizedActivation_i = \frac{\MiniBatchActivation_i - \BatchNormMean}{\sqrt{\BatchNormStandardDeviation^2 + \NumericalStabilityConstant}} \tag{7}\] \[ \BatchNormOutput_i = \NormalizationScale\NormalizedActivation_i + \NormalizationShift \tag{8}\]

The parameters \(\NormalizationScale\) (scale) and \(\NormalizationShift\) (shift) are learned during training, while \(\NumericalStabilityConstant\) (typically \(10^{-5}\)) prevents division by zero. The affine transform restores learned scale and shift flexibility, but fixed \(\NormalizationScale\) and \(\NormalizationShift\) cannot exactly invert varying statistics for every batch. Normalization permits larger learning rates by preventing intermediate activations from exploding into saturating non-linear regimes, accelerating convergence relative to unnormalized networks.

While batch normalization enabled training of much deeper CNNs, it needs batch-wide statistics, which constrains distributed and sequence workloads. When a mini-batch is split across several accelerators, computing true mini-batch statistics requires an all-reduce, a collective that sums every accelerator’s partial statistics and returns the total to all of them, adding communication latency to every normalized layer. If instead computed locally on small per-accelerator batches, noisy empirical statistics destabilize optimization. Furthermore, batch normalization cannot handle variable-length sequences or text generation, which produces one token at a time, without complex padding masks and training-serving skew. Layer normalization resolves these limitations by normalizing across the feature dimension rather than across the batch (Ba et al. 2016).

Ba, Jimmy Lei, Jamie Ryan Kiros, and Geoffrey E. Hinton. 2016. “Layer Normalization.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1607.06450.

↪ The AllReduce collective and its communication cost are developed in Data parallelism.

For an input vector \(\LayerInputVector\in \mathbb{R}^{\HiddenDimension}\) with \(\HiddenDimension\) features, layer normalization first computes the mean and variance across those \(\HiddenDimension\) components: \[ \LayerNormMean= \frac{1}{\HiddenDimension}\sum_{i=1}^{\HiddenDimension} \LayerInputComponent_i \qquad \LayerNormStandardDeviation^2 = \frac{1}{\HiddenDimension}\sum_{i=1}^{\HiddenDimension} (\LayerInputComponent_i - \LayerNormMean)^2 \]

With those per-token statistics in hand, equation 9 defines the complete layer normalization operation, where \(\odot\) denotes element-wise multiplication: \[ \text{LayerNorm}(\LayerInputVector) = \frac{\LayerInputVector- \LayerNormMean}{\sqrt{\LayerNormStandardDeviation^2 + \NumericalStabilityConstant}} \odot \NormalizationScaleVector+ \NormalizationShiftVector \tag{9}\]

Because each token or sample is normalized independently, the computation stays within an accelerator’s on-chip SRAM and registers, with no cross-sample or cross-device communication. That independence makes layer normalization the standard choice for transformers, where sequences vary in length and text generation evaluates a single token at a time. Table 4 contrasts the three variants along the machine and operational dimensions that decide between them.

The computational profile of normalization is defined by its arithmetic intensity. Because normalization applies elementwise operations to large activation tensors, execution is memory-bandwidth bound rather than compute bound. A straightforward LayerNorm kernel makes two reductions across the hidden dimension \(\HiddenDimension\), one for the mean \(\LayerNormMean\) and one for the variance \(\LayerNormStandardDeviation^2\), although Welford’s algorithm can compute both in a single pass. RMSNorm drops the mean-centering reduction, leaving a single sum of squares, which simplifies the kernel and eases fusion with neighboring operations; its authors report 7–64 percent lower running time across the models they tested.

↪ Kernel fusion, which collapses memory-bound normalization and activation chains into single kernels whose intermediates stay on chip, is developed in The memory wall and kernel fusion.

The table’s last row is an operational cost. BatchNorm keeps nonlearned running mean and variance buffers and switches from mini-batch statistics in training to those running statistics at inference, so a mismatch between the buffers and deployment data is a frequent source of training-serving skew. LayerNorm and RMSNorm compute identically in both modes and carry no running state. With skip connections and normalization in place, depth stops being an optimization barrier.

Checkpoint 6.2: Making depth trainable

Depth extends how far a network’s view reaches across its input, but a convolutional stack still sees only its receptive field, whether that field spans pixels or the time bins of a spectrogram. A streaming system that must carry information across variable time spans within a fixed state budget motivates a different trade-off: updating a compact recurrent state as each observation arrives.

Self-Check: Question

  1. In a residual block implementing \(\mathbf{y} = \mathcal{F}(\mathbf{x}) + \mathbf{x}\), how does the additive identity shortcut mathematically improve gradient conditioning through the layer Jacobian \(\mathbf{J} = \frac{\partial \mathbf{y}}{\partial \mathbf{x}}\) during backpropagation in very deep networks?

    1. The shortcut forces the residual function \(\mathcal{F}(\mathbf{x})\) to have zero weights, turning the network into an immutable linear identity operator.
    2. The Jacobian takes the form \(\mathbf{J} = \mathbf{I} + \frac{\partial \mathcal{F}}{\partial \mathbf{x}}\), so when residual-branch derivatives are small the block stays near identity and gradients have a direct additive path, improving conditioning without guaranteeing exact unit gain at arbitrary depth.
    3. The shortcut doubles the singular values of the weight matrix at every layer, ensuring gradients explode exponentially rather than vanish.
    4. The shortcut eliminates the backpropagation chain rule by replacing gradient updates with forward-only finite differences.

  2. Compare Batch Normalization (BatchNorm) and Layer Normalization (LayerNorm) along two critical systems dimensions: (a) sensitivity to mini-batch size during training, and (b) operational differences between training and inference (including training-serving skew).

See Answers →

6.5 RNNs: Sequential Pattern Processing

An MLP requires a fixed-width input vector and a CNN sees only a bounded spatial window, so neither can process a continuous audio stream or a sentence of arbitrary length without padding, truncating, or stacking layers until activation memory overflows. Where convolutional networks exploit spatial structure, many real-world signals carry temporal structure, in which sequential order and prior context determine meaning. Recurrent networks process these variable-length streams within a fixed memory budget by carrying and updating a persistent state vector from one time step to the next.

Definition 6.4: Recurrent neural networks

Recurrent neural networks (RNNs) are sequence-processing architectures that maintain a hidden state \(\RecurrentHiddenState_t = \LayerFunction(\RecurrentHiddenState_{t-1}, \LayerInputVector_t)\) updated at each time step, encoding the assumption that the current output depends on all prior inputs through this fixed-size state vector.

  1. Significance: The fixed-size state provides \(\mathcal{O}(1)\) inference state memory regardless of sequence length (processing a 10,000-token sequence requires the same state memory as a 10-token sequence), but the sequential update rule forces all \(\SequenceLength\) recurrent transitions to execute in order. Separate per-step kernels (compiled accelerator routines, a different sense of the word from a convolution kernel) add launch overhead \((\Latency)\); fusion can reduce those launches while preserving the serial dependency chain.
  2. Distinction: Unlike MLPs, which require a fixed-dimensional input vector, or CNNs, which restrict context to a fixed spatial window, RNNs compress an unbounded history into a fixed-size bottleneck state, so they accept sequences of any length without widening the input layer. Gradient signals pass back through the recurrent steps, where repeated Jacobian products can attenuate or amplify them (derived below).
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that RNNs are obsolete. For streaming inference on resource-constrained hardware, such as keyword spotting on a microcontroller, a model that must keep every past input to see the whole stream needs memory that grows with stream length, which can be prohibitive. An RNN’s \(\mathcal{O}(1)\) state size makes it a candidate when a fixed state budget is required.

RNNs hold state memory fixed, but latency grows with sequence length.

Elman, Jeffrey L. 1990. “Finding Structure in Time.” Cognitive Science 14 (2): 179–211. https://doi.org/10.1207/s15516709cog1402_1.

Recurrent architectures, from simple recurrent networks to gated variants such as long short-term memory (LSTM) networks (Elman 1990; Hochreiter and Schmidhuber 1997), encode a temporal inductive bias in which execution order follows causal time and history compresses into that recurring state. Rather than scaling parameter count or activation storage with dilated or deeply stacked windows when relevant tokens sit far apart, the network reuses one transition operator at every step. That choice trades parallelism for bounded state: it provides variable-length temporal context within a fixed memory footprint, but it introduces loop-carried data dependencies that serialize execution on parallel accelerator hardware.

6.5.1 Pattern processing needs

A word like “bank” cannot be disambiguated on its own: “river bank” refers to a shoreline, whereas “investment bank” refers to a financial institution. Only the preceding words resolve which meaning applies. Acoustic waveforms and sensor telemetry behave the same way: a single audio sample or temperature reading is ambiguous without the history that came before it.

Data with this sequential structure imposes three requirements on a model:

  1. Variable-length inputs: Sequences arrive with different lengths \(\SequenceLength\), ruling out a single fixed-width input layer.
  2. Long-range memory: The model must carry relevant signals across many time steps without requiring a fixed window size.
  3. Continuous state updates: Internal representations must update as each new token arrives, folding in new evidence while dropping stale history.

Meeting all three streaming requirements without growing the model alongside the input length requires turning the feedforward graph into a state machine whose output at step \(\TimeStepIndex-1\) feeds step \(\TimeStepIndex\).

6.5.2 Algorithmic structure

To meet the requirements of section 6.5.1, recurrent neural networks introduce directed cycles in their computational graph. Rather than mapping inputs directly to outputs through feedforward layers, an RNN maintains a persistent vector, the hidden state, that updates iteratively at each time step. The hidden state is a running summary of the past.

The fundamental state transition in equation 10 computes the current hidden state \(\RecurrentHiddenState_t\) by combining the incoming token vector \(\LayerInputVector_t\) with the preceding state \(\RecurrentHiddenState_{t-1}\): \[ \RecurrentHiddenState_t = \LayerNonlinearity(\RecurrentStateWeights\RecurrentHiddenState_{t-1} + \RecurrentInputWeights\LayerInputVector_t + \RecurrentHiddenBias) \tag{10}\] where \(\RecurrentHiddenState_t\) denotes the hidden state at time \(\TimeStepIndex\), \(\LayerInputVector_t\) denotes the input at time \(\TimeStepIndex\), \(\RecurrentStateWeights\) contains the recurrent weights, \(\RecurrentInputWeights\) contains the input weights, \(\RecurrentHiddenBias\) is the hidden-state bias vector, and \(\LayerNonlinearity\) is the activation function. The equation uses column vectors; the later listings use row-major batched tensors, so their weight multiplications appear on the right. Unrolled across time (figure 8, right), the loop becomes a chain of transitions \(\RecurrentHiddenState_{t-1} \to \RecurrentHiddenState_t \to \RecurrentHiddenState_{t+1}\) in which every link reuses the same recurrent weight matrix \(\RecurrentStateWeights\), so no step can begin until the one before it has finished.

Unfolding the recurrent loop across \(\SequenceLength\) steps reveals the algorithmic properties of recurrence. Parameter count remains independent of sequence length \(\SequenceLength\) because the weight matrices \(\RecurrentStateWeights\) and \(\RecurrentInputWeights\) are shared across all time steps. However, sharing weights across time establishes a loop-carried dependency: step \(\TimeStepIndex\) cannot evaluate \(\RecurrentHiddenState_t\) until step \(\TimeStepIndex-1\) finishes producing \(\RecurrentHiddenState_{t-1}\). This dependency forms a critical path of length \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength)\) serial operations that prevents hardware accelerators from parallelizing across the sequence dimension.

This sequential structure produces different memory characteristics in inference and in training. During streaming inference, an RNN evaluates one step at a time, discarding prior raw inputs and retaining only the current hidden state. State memory overhead remains strictly \(\mathcal{O}(\HiddenDimension)\) in the hidden dimension \(\HiddenDimension\), regardless of whether the sequence spans 10 or 10,000 steps. In contrast, training via backpropagation through time (BPTT) (Werbos 1990) unrolls the graph across the entire sequence of length \(\SequenceLength\). Computing gradients via the chain rule requires evaluating the Jacobian product across all intermediate transitions: \[ \frac{\partial \LossFunction}{\partial \RecurrentHiddenState_0} = \frac{\partial \LossFunction}{\partial \FinalRecurrentHiddenState} \prod_{t=1}^{\SequenceLength} \frac{\partial \RecurrentHiddenState_t}{\partial \RecurrentHiddenState_{t-1}} \] Ordinary BPTT retains the intermediate activations \(\RecurrentHiddenState_1, \RecurrentHiddenState_2, \dots, \FinalRecurrentHiddenState\) for these Jacobian products. This retained-state scheme converts an \(\mathcal{O}(\HiddenDimension)\) runtime state into an \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength \cdot \HiddenDimension)\) training footprint; recomputing saved intermediates trades additional work for memory. Because every factor in the product combines activation derivatives with \(\RecurrentStateWeights\), the backpropagated signal attenuates or amplifies exponentially over long horizons, preventing a plain RNN from learning dependencies across more than a few dozen time steps unless the recurrence gives the state a selective bypass around \(\RecurrentStateWeights\).

Werbos, Paul J. 1990. “Backpropagation Through Time: What It Does and How to Do It.” Proceedings of the IEEE 78 (10): 1550–60. https://doi.org/10.1109/5.58337.

6.5.3 Gated recurrence

Gated recurrent architectures prevent long-horizon gradients from vanishing by replacing the unconditional state overwrite of equation 10 with learned gates, elementwise valves that decide at each step how much of the stored state to keep, overwrite, or expose. Each gate applies a sigmoid to a learned projection of the current input and previous state, producing values between zero and one that multiply the quantity it controls: a value near one passes that quantity through, and a value near zero blocks it. LSTMs19 (Hochreiter and Schmidhuber 1997) maintain a separate cell state beside the hidden state and govern it with input, forget, and output gates plus a candidate update. When the forget gate stays near one, the cell state passes from step to step almost unchanged, giving gradients a path across long horizons without being multiplied by \(\RecurrentStateWeights\) at every transition. GRUs20 (Cho et al. 2014) merge the same mechanism into two gates. For the machine, an LSTM step computes four affine transformations of the input and previous state instead of one, so it holds four times the recurrent weights, performs four times the multiply-accumulates per step, and carries twice the state (hidden and cell). Runtimes fuse the four projections into one matrix multiply four times as wide, which raises its arithmetic intensity without shortening the serial chain of steps.

19 LSTM (long short-term memory): Invented by Hochreiter and Schmidhuber in 1997, LSTMs introduced a “Constant Error Carousel,” a gated cell state that protects error signals from exponential decay during backpropagation through time. The systems cost is that a standard LSTM computes input, forget, and output gates plus a candidate cell update, giving roughly four affine transformations per time step vs. one in a vanilla RNN. Transformers replaced LSTMs in many large-scale language workloads not because attention costs less arithmetic, since it costs more, but because it parallelizes across sequence positions where a recurrence cannot.

Hochreiter, Sepp, and Jürgen Schmidhuber. 1997. “Long Short-Term Memory.” Neural Computation 9 (8): 1735–80. https://doi.org/10.1162/neco.1997.9.8.1735.

20 GRU (gated recurrent unit): Cho et al. (2014) describes a gated hidden unit for encoder-decoder translation that uses reset and update gates to control how the hidden state is updated. Relative to an LSTM’s input, forget, and output gates plus candidate cell update, this gives a simpler gated recurrence. The broader systems lesson: architectural simplification can reduce state and matrix operations when it preserves task performance, a principle that recurs in efficiency-oriented designs from MobileNet to distilled transformers.

Cho, Kyunghyun, Bart van Merrienboer, Dzmitry Bahdanau, and Yoshua Bengio. 2014. “On the Properties of Neural Machine Translation: Encoder-Decoder Approaches.” Proceedings of SSST-8, Eighth Workshop on Syntax, Semantics and Structure in Statistical Translation, 103–11. https://doi.org/10.3115/v1/w14-4012.

6.5.4 Computational mapping

In hardware, a recurrent step is two matrix products followed by an elementwise update. Convolution reuses each weight across a grid of positions available all at once (section 6.3.4.3); a recurrent step reuses its weights only across time, and each step must wait for the state the previous step produces.

Listing 5 demonstrates the single-time-step mechanism using framework-level matrix operations: combine the previous hidden state with the current input, add the bias, and apply the activation to produce the next hidden state. The code is intentionally local to one step because the systems cost is not the step itself, but the dependency chain that prevents parallel execution across time.

At the framework level, rnn_layer_step hides the underlying loop structure and memory traffic behind high-level matrix multiplications (matmul). In hardware, evaluating this step requires moving weights and activations through the memory hierarchy. To see where execution serializes and where parallelism remains, listing 6 expands the matrix operations into explicit loop nests down to scalar multiply-accumulate operations.

The loop structure exposes the concurrency boundaries of recurrent execution. Batch instances in Loop 1 are independent, so sequences within a batch parallelize across accelerator cores without cross-talk. Within each batch instance, Loops 2 and 3 execute the recurrent transformation (\(\RecurrentHiddenState_{t-1}\RecurrentStateWeights\)) and input projection (\(\LayerInputVector_t\RecurrentInputWeights\)). Both reductions must accumulate fully before Loop 4 can apply the elementwise bias addition and activation function to produce \(\RecurrentHiddenState_t\).

For an input dimension of 100 and a hidden state dimension of 128, each step executes two matrix multiplications: a \(100{\times}128\) input projection and a \(128{\times}128\) recurrent transformation. When batch size is large (\(\BatchSize\gg 1\)), these operations execute as general matrix-matrix multiplications (GEMM) that reuse each loaded weight across all \(\BatchSize\) samples. When batch size is small (\(\BatchSize=1\), typical of real-time streaming inference), they collapse into the same weight-streaming matrix-vector products as the batch-one dense layer of equation 2, which starve accelerator compute units.

6.5.5 System implications

The hard physical constraint that recurrent architectures impose is sequential dependency. In the update \(\RecurrentHiddenState_t = \tanh(\RecurrentStateWeights\RecurrentHiddenState_{t-1} + \RecurrentInputWeights\LayerInputVector_t)\), the recurrent transformation at time step \(\TimeStepIndex\) needs the state produced by step \(\TimeStepIndex-1\). An input sequence of length \(\SequenceLength= 1{,}000\) therefore requires \(1{,}000\) ordered state updates, even when a fused kernel executes several or all updates within one launch. Hardware can parallelize across batch instances and within each matrix operation; input projections can also be computed across time when the inputs are available. Faster arithmetic or memory shortens individual steps, while the recurrent dependency chain remains.

During inference, recurrent networks need little state memory. Because the recurrent state compresses history into a fixed-size vector \(\RecurrentHiddenState_t\), inference state memory scales as \(\mathcal{O}(\HiddenDimension)\) (for example, 2 KB for a 512-dimensional FP32 state), remaining completely flat whether sequence length \(\SequenceLength\) is 10 or 10,000 tokens. This constant memory footprint contrasts sharply with attention mechanisms (section 6.6), whose stored context grows with every token generated, an \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength\cdot \HiddenDimension)\) store that the transformer section sizes. For streaming deployments on memory-constrained edge hardware, such as microcontrollers, an RNN’s \(\mathcal{O}(1)\) runtime state avoids the growing context store that limits attention-based models.

Recurrent execution splits the memory hierarchy between two behaviors: high temporal locality for parameters, and low operational intensity under standard framework dispatch. Weight matrices \(\RecurrentStateWeights\) and \(\RecurrentInputWeights\) are reused at every time step, residing comfortably in SRAM or L2 cache if the model fits on-chip. However, naively launching separate kernels for input projection, recurrent projection, bias addition, and activation at each time step incurs severe kernel-launch overhead and forces intermediate tensors back and forth across HBM. To mitigate this memory-bandwidth bottleneck, production recurrent runtimes fuse the projections, bias addition, and activation into a single monolithic kernel that maintains \(\RecurrentHiddenState_t\) within GPU registers and shared memory throughout the sequence execution. At batch one, each recurrent step is the dense layer’s matrix-vector product again, with the same energy profile. Unless the recurrent weights stay on chip from step to step, every step pays DRAM energy for every weight, about 139× the energy of its arithmetic, which is a second reason streaming runtimes keep those weights resident.

↪ What a fused kernel that holds its state in registers and shared memory costs in on-chip capacity and occupancy is developed in Kernel fusion on the accelerator.

Checkpoint 6.3: Recurrent state and the serial chain

Recurrence trades parallelism for a fixed memory budget. Check your understanding:

This architectural tension between fixed-capacity state and serialized execution defined sequence processing before transformers. While recurrent networks maintain constant memory overhead regardless of sequence length, the sequential dependency prevents hardware from parallelizing across time steps. The fixed-capacity state also becomes an information bottleneck in which early inputs fade over long spans, and gradients through long chains of recurrent steps vanish or explode (the vanishing gradient problem) (Bengio et al. 1994). State-space models such as Mamba (Gu and Dao 2023) later revived recurrence for exactly its systems strength, a fixed-size state at decode time where an attention model’s stored context grows, while training with a parallel scan instead of a step-by-step loop. Overcoming the serial chain and the fading state in general, however, required an architecture capable of establishing direct, content-addressable links across arbitrary sequence positions without intervening recurrent steps: the attention mechanism (section 6.6).

Bengio, Yoshua, Patrice Simard, and Paolo Frasconi. 1994. “Learning Long-Term Dependencies with Gradient Descent Is Difficult.” IEEE Transactions on Neural Networks 5 (2): 157–66. https://doi.org/10.1109/72.279181.
Gu, Albert, and Tri Dao. 2023. “Mamba: Linear-Time Sequence Modeling with Selective State Spaces.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2312.00752.
Self-Check: Question

  1. An RNN processes a sequence of length \(S = 1{,}000\) tokens with hidden state dimension \(d_{\text{hidden}} = 128\). Which statement correctly describes the scaling of its inference state memory versus its training activation memory?

    1. Inference state memory is \(\mathcal{O}(d_{\text{hidden}})\) (constant \(\mathcal{O}(1)\) with respect to sequence length \(S\)), whereas training with backpropagation through time (BPTT) requires storing activations across all steps, scaling as \(\mathcal{O}(S \cdot d_{\text{hidden}})\) under ordinary retained-state BPTT.
    2. Both inference state memory and training activation memory scale quadratically as \(\mathcal{O}(S^2)\) due to recurrent hidden-to-hidden weight matrices.
    3. Inference state memory scales linearly as \(\mathcal{O}(S \cdot d_{\text{hidden}})\), while training memory is constant because weights are shared across all time steps.
    4. Inference requires zero memory because recurrent states are discarded immediately after computing output probabilities.

  2. Explain why upgrading an accelerator from 10 TFLOP/s to 100 TFLOP/s can reduce the elapsed time per recurrent step in an RNN processing a single long sequence, but cannot reduce the number of ordered state updates on the critical dependency chain. Contrast this with the parallel sequence processing capability of a transformer.

  3. Because transformers offer superior parallelization and representational capacity for long-range dependencies, recurrent neural networks are entirely obsolete and have no valid deployment use cases in modern ML systems.

  4. Order the mathematical and dataflow operations executed during a single time-step forward pass of a standard Elman RNN cell:

  1. Multiply the previous hidden state vector \(\mathbf{h}_{t-1}\) by the recurrent weight matrix \(\mathbf{W}_{\text{hh}}\)
  2. Multiply the current input vector \(\mathbf{x}_t\) by the input weight matrix \(\mathbf{W}_{\text{hx}}\)
  3. Sum the recurrent contribution, input contribution, and hidden bias vector \(\mathbf{b}_h\)
  4. Apply the nonlinear activation function (e.g., \(\tanh\)) to generate the new hidden state \(\mathbf{h}_t\)
  5. Multiply the new hidden state \(\mathbf{h}_t\) by the output weight matrix \(\mathbf{W}_{\text{yh}}\) to produce output \(\mathbf{y}_t\)

  1. During backpropagation through time (BPTT) over \(S\) time steps, the gradient of the loss with respect to the initial hidden state satisfies \(\frac{\partial \mathcal{L}}{\partial \mathbf{h}_0} \propto \prod_{t=1}^S \frac{\partial \mathbf{h}_t}{\partial \mathbf{h}_{t-1}}\). Explain the mathematical mechanism that causes gradients to vanish or explode as \(S\) grows large.

  2. In a standard RNN layer with input dimension \(d_{\text{in}} = 100\) and hidden state dimension \(d_{\text{hidden}} = 128\), how many total multiply-accumulate (MAC) operations are performed per sequence step to compute the unactivated hidden state?

    1. 12,800 MACs, because only the input projection performs matrix multiplication.
    2. 29,184 MACs, consisting of \(128 \times 128 = 16{,}384\text{ MACs}\) for the recurrent projection plus \(100 \times 128 = 12{,}800\text{ MACs}\) for the input projection.
    3. 1,280,000 MACs, because recurrence multiplies all hidden states across all past time steps simultaneously.
    4. 256 MACs, because an RNN updates only a single vector addition per step.

See Answers →

6.6 Attention: Dynamic Processing

Recurrent networks serialize execution along the sequence dimension and compress all historical context into a fixed-capacity hidden state vector (section 6.5.5). In a sentence such as “The cat, which was sitting by the window overlooking the garden, was sleeping,” the subject “cat” and predicate “sleeping” are separated by ten intervening words, yet they form the core syntactic and semantic relationship. An RNN must process all intervening tokens sequentially, risking information loss across intermediate recurrent transitions while preventing hardware from parallelizing across time steps. This constraint motivates an alternative computational pattern: an operation that directly computes relevance between any two positions regardless of distance.

Attention mechanisms21 address this limitation (Bahdanau et al. 2015) by introducing dynamic connectivity patterns that adapt to input content. The setting was sequence-to-sequence translation, built as an encoder-decoder pair. An encoder network reads the input sentence into one representation per input position, and a decoder network generates the output sentence one token at a time, carrying its own recurrent decoder state. Recurrent versions of that pair passed only the encoder’s final state to the decoder. Rather than forcing all context through that single fixed-capacity vector, encoder-decoder attention computes similarity scores between the decoder state at each output step and all encoder positions, routing information dynamically across the sequence. A single decoder step compares one query with every encoder position. In full-sequence self-attention, each position queries all positions in the same sequence, so the number of score interactions grows quadratically with sequence length.

21 Bahdanau attention: This approach broke the “fixed-length vector” bottleneck of prior sequence-to-sequence models. The core trade-off was accepting a linear, \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength)\) memory cost to store all input states in exchange for overcoming the information loss inherent in a single vector.

Definition 6.5: Attention mechanisms

Attention mechanisms are neural network operations that compute a weighted sum of value vectors, where the weights are derived from learned similarity scores between a query vector and a set of key vectors, enabling dynamic, content-dependent information routing between any two positions in a sequence.

  1. Significance: Attention connects any two tokens in \(\mathcal{O}(1)\) depth but computes \(\SequenceLength^2\) score interactions. If those scores are materialized, a 4,096-token sequence with 16-bit scores consumes 33.6 MB per layer per head (about 16.8M scores at 2 bytes each), directly increasing the iron law’s \(\DataVolume\) term.
  2. Distinction: Unlike RNNs, which compress all prior context into a single fixed-size state vector, attention mechanisms retain every token’s representation and compare tokens directly. When a model generates text one token at a time, it keeps the key and value vectors of earlier tokens rather than recomputing them, and that key-value (KV) cache grows as \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength \HiddenDimension)\); the transformer section explains generation and sizes the cache.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that attention is a general-purpose weighting scheme that can be applied freely. Quadratic score computation is a hard scaling constraint: doubling the context quadruples score interactions. Naive implementations also quadruple score storage; tiled exact algorithms such as FlashAttention avoid the full matrix, while sparse variants reduce the scores computed (Child et al. 2019).
Child, Rewon, Scott Gray, Alec Radford, and Ilya Sutskever. 2019. “Generating Long Sequences with Sparse Transformers.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1904.10509.

22 Transformer: The founding paper, “Attention Is All You Need,” made the explicit systems claim that a parallel attention mechanism could fully replace sequential recurrent processing. This architectural trade eliminates an RNN’s \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength)\) path length constraint on parallelism but introduces \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength^2)\) dense score computation, a quadratic cost that continues to shape context-window engineering.

The ability of attention to connect any position to any other made the recurrent structure unnecessary for many sequence tasks. The transformer22 (Vaswani et al. 2017) removed recurrence entirely, trading the RNN’s \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength)\) sequential path for constant path length between positions within one attention layer and thereby enabling parallelization across sequence positions on high-throughput accelerators.

Attention functions as a computational primitive rather than an independent architecture family. The transformer (section 6.7), the family built around it, inherits its core systems properties directly from attention: content-dependent routing, parallel sequence execution, and quadratic score scaling. Evaluating those properties begins with the dynamic pattern-processing problem that attention addresses.

6.6.1 Pattern processing needs

In “the trophy did not fit in the suitcase because it was too big,” the word “it” refers to “trophy,” but if “big” changes to “small,” “it” refers to “suitcase.” The same positions, at the same distances, need different links depending on what the words mean. Neither a CNN’s fixed spatial window nor an RNN’s step-by-step hidden state can route information directly between arbitrary positions based on content. Dynamic sequence processing needs an operation that scores relationships from the tokens themselves, letting each word draw directly on the words that matter to it regardless of how far apart they sit (figure 9).

The bipartite connection lines in figure 9 illustrate how the pronoun “they” could receive information from “student” and “finish.” The hypothetical weights demonstrate a direct routing path, not a measured coreference result. Pairwise connections can be evaluated in parallel, allowing a single layer to connect these positions without propagating intermediate state across intervening tokens.

Input-dependent processing extends well beyond language. In protein structure prediction, amino-acid interactions depend on chemical properties and spatial arrangement, not only chain position; in document analysis, connections between sections depend on semantic content rather than proximity. Graph convolutional networks (GCNs) (Kipf and Welling 2017) share attention’s input-dependent routing but gather features along the sparse edges of each input graph, producing irregular memory access that can bind execution on memory bandwidth and cache misses rather than compute throughput.

Kipf, Thomas N, and Max Welling. 2017. “Semi-Supervised Classification with Graph Convolutional Networks.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

Input dependence does not imply all-to-all connectivity. Dense attention scores every pair of sequence elements, whereas a GCN restricts aggregation to edges in the observed graph. The common systems challenge is that the computation or memory-access pattern responds to the input rather than following a fixed stencil. Even when tensor dimensions stay fixed, content-dependent scores determine which information is emphasized. Attention provides one mechanism for learning such relationships, and dense self-attention forms the foundation of the transformer architecture.

6.6.2 Algorithmic structure

To route information dynamically (section 6.6.1), the network needs an operator that turns a variable-length sequence into content-dependent weights (Bahdanau et al. 2015). The transformer architecture computes these weights with the scaled dot-product attention operator introduced by Vaswani et al. (2017): \[ \text{Attention}(\AttentionQueryMatrix, \AttentionKeyMatrix, \AttentionValuesMatrix) = \text{softmax} \left(\frac{\AttentionQueryMatrix\AttentionKeyMatrix^T}{\sqrt{\AttentionHeadDimension}}\right)\AttentionValuesMatrix\]

23 Query-key-value (QKV): The terminology is borrowed from information retrieval, explaining why the equation uses three distinct learned projections to calculate pairwise scores. Creating these projections requires three independent weight matrices, costing \(3 \times \HiddenDimension^2\) parameters per layer. Storing the key and value projections of earlier tokens is what produces the key-value (KV) cache of autoregressive serving (section 6.7.4.2).

For this self-attention case, the inputs \(\AttentionQueryMatrix\) (queries), \(\AttentionKeyMatrix\) (keys), and \(\AttentionValuesMatrix\) (values)23 are learned linear projections of the token representations (\(\AttentionQueryMatrix= \LayerInputMatrix\QueryProjectionMatrix\), \(\AttentionKeyMatrix= \LayerInputMatrix\KeyProjectionMatrix\), \(\AttentionValuesMatrix= \LayerInputMatrix\AttentionValuesProjectionMatrix\)). Attention uses three separate projection matrices rather than computing dot-product affinities directly between raw tokens (\(\LayerInputMatrix\LayerInputMatrix^T\)) for two architectural reasons:

  1. Asymmetric relationships: Direct token dot products \(\LayerInputVector_i \cdot \LayerInputVector_j\) are symmetric (\(\LayerInputVector_i \cdot \LayerInputVector_j = \LayerInputVector_j \cdot \LayerInputVector_i\)), whereas relationships in language are not. In “the chef cooked the meal,” the verb “cooked” looks for its direct object (“meal”), while “meal” plays a different role, so the two directions of the pair need different scores. Splitting the projection into a query (what a token looks for) and a key (what a token offers) lets \(\CookedTokenQueryVector\cdot \MealTokenKeyVector\) be high while \(\MealTokenQueryVector\cdot \CookedTokenKeyVector\) stays low.
  2. Separating where to look from what to pass on: The dot products \(\AttentionQueryMatrix\AttentionKeyMatrix^T\) decide where to look (routing weights), while the value projection \(\AttentionValuesMatrix\) decides what information to retrieve. Without a separate value projection, a token that serves mainly as a structural marker (such as a preposition or punctuation mark) would pass its entire raw embedding into later layers. Projecting through \(\AttentionValuesProjectionMatrix\) lets the network strip away surface token identity and pass along only the features downstream layers need.

For an input sequence of length \(\SequenceLength\) projected to a dimension \(\AttentionHeadDimension\) (here the full width \(\HiddenDimension\); the transformer’s multi-head attention later splits that width across several parallel attention computations called heads), the dot product \(\AttentionQueryMatrix\AttentionKeyMatrix^T\) computes all \(\SequenceLength^2\) pairwise token similarities. Scaling by \(1/\sqrt{\AttentionHeadDimension}\) prevents dot products from growing large in magnitude at high dimensions, which would push softmax into regions with vanishingly small gradients. The softmax operator24 normalizes each row of the score matrix into a probability distribution over key positions. Finally, multiplying these normalized weights by \(\AttentionValuesMatrix\) produces output vectors formed as weighted combinations of the value representations.

24 Softmax: Named as a “soft” (differentiable) version of argmax, with mathematical roots in Boltzmann’s statistical mechanics, softmax normalizes each query row over all \(\SequenceLength\) scores and therefore requires a reduction over each row. Online softmax lets that reduction run over tiles with running statistics, which is why it need not force quadratic storage (section 6.6.4.1).

↪ The dot product as a similarity measure is collected for reference in The dot product as similarity.

Figure 10 traces this dataflow for a sequence of length \(\SequenceLength= 6\): cyan query rows match against red key columns to populate the \(6{\times}6\) similarity matrix, whose rows subsequently weight the green value vectors to produce the contextualized output vectors on the right. Each row of the resulting \(\SequenceLength{\times}\SequenceLength\) matrix determines how strongly an individual token attends to every token in the sequence. In a trained model, the largest affinity values reflect strong contextual dependencies, while near-zero entries suppress irrelevant positions.

While attention weights vary dynamically with the input tokens, generating the query, key, and value vectors requires static linear projections against learned parameter matrices. In accelerator implementations, these three operations are fused into a single batched matrix multiplication to maximize Tensor Core saturation and reduce kernel launch overhead. Packing the three weight matrices side by side turns three projections into one (figure 11). The \(6{\times}768\) token embedding matrix (one learned 768-value vector per token) multiplies a \(768{\times}2304\) weight matrix whose three 768-column blocks hold the query, key, and value weights. Adding the bias yields a \(6{\times}2304\) output that contains all three projections, computed in a single kernel pass.

6.6.3 Computational mapping

Lowering the attention operator \(\text{Attention}(\AttentionQueryMatrix, \AttentionKeyMatrix, \AttentionValuesMatrix) = \text{softmax}(\AttentionQueryMatrix\AttentionKeyMatrix^T/\sqrt{\AttentionHeadDimension})\AttentionValuesMatrix\) to physical execution exposes the system cost of data-dependent connectivity. Evaluating pairwise alignments requires two distinct matrix multiplications per attention head: the score GEMM (\(\AttentionQueryMatrix\AttentionKeyMatrix^T\)) and the context GEMM (\(\AttentionWeightMatrix\AttentionValuesMatrix\), where \(\AttentionWeightMatrix= \text{softmax}(\AttentionQueryMatrix\AttentionKeyMatrix^T/\sqrt{\AttentionHeadDimension})\)). For an input of sequence length \(\SequenceLength\) and head dimension \(\AttentionHeadDimension\), each phase executes \(\SequenceLength^2 \AttentionHeadDimension\) multiply-accumulate operations, yielding \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength^2 \AttentionHeadDimension)\) computational complexity per head.

Listing 7 contrasts the high-level matrix formulation with an explicit loop implementation that exposes this quadratic scaling. The matrix version lowers to optimized GEMM routines on accelerator Tensor Cores, whereas the loop version reveals the underlying physical execution structure: batch iterations, quadratic query-key inner products, row-wise softmax reductions, and the final value accumulation pass across all sequence positions.

6.6.4 System implications

Attention eliminates the latency-bound serial path of RNNs, but a naive implementation replaces it with an \(\SequenceLength{\times}\SequenceLength\) attention-score matrix that grows quadratically with sequence length \(\SequenceLength\). In iron law terms, dense attention trades serial recurrent steps for pairwise score arithmetic and heavy intermediate data movement. Some of these costs are inherent to computing all-to-all interactions; others come only from materializing the full score matrix in memory.

Memory requirements

Materializing an \(\SequenceLength{\times}\SequenceLength\) attention-weight matrix for every sequence and head creates a quadratic memory bottleneck that quickly dwarfs both the \(\SequenceLength{\times}\HiddenDimension\) activations and the fixed-size projection weights on long inputs. Tiled exact-attention kernels such as FlashAttention stream score blocks through on-chip SRAM and avoid writing the full score matrix to HBM. They still compute all \(\SequenceLength^2\) dense interactions (FlashAttention: IO-aware attention optimization develops the I/O-aware tiling in full, and Tiled attention and online softmax collects the online softmax recurrence for reference). This distinction separates the unavoidable quadratic compute cost of dense attention from the avoidable quadratic memory footprint of storing the full score matrix in HBM.

Doubling context quadruples score interactions and naive score storage.

Napkin Math 6.1: The quadratic bottleneck
Problem: How much memory does a materialized attention matrix require at sequence length \(\SequenceLength =\) 100,000 (context window)?

Math:

  1. Matrix size: The attention score matrix \((\AttentionQueryMatrix\AttentionKeyMatrix^T)\) has dimensions \(\SequenceLength{\times}\SequenceLength\) per head, across \(\AttentionHeadCount\) heads (12 heads in this example).
  2. Elements: 100,000 \(\times\) 100,000 \(\times\) 12 heads = 1.2 × 10¹¹ elements.
  3. Memory: At FP16 (2 bytes/element): 1.2 × 10¹¹ \(\times\) 2 bytes = 240 GB.
  4. Retained layers: 240 GB per layer \(\times\) 32 retained layers = 7,680 GB.

Systems insight: A materialized attention matrix consumes 240 GB per layer. Naive training that retains all 32 layers’ scores for backward would require 7,680 GB, far exceeding any single GPU’s capacity. This memory wall motivates two broad implementation strategies: avoid materializing the full matrix by tiling the computation, or reduce the number of scores computed in the first place.

Computation and data movement

Attention computation splits into two primary GEMM phases interleaved by a row-wise reduction: generating pairwise query-key scores and computing weighted sums over values. Query-key inner products require \(\BatchSize \times \AttentionHeadCount \times \SequenceLength^2 \times \AttentionHeadDimension\) MAC operations, mirrored symmetrically by the value-aggregation stage. The intervening softmax requires row-wise maximum finding, exponentiation, and summation. Unlike static weight convolutions where filter weights are reused across fixed grid positions, attention computes transient, input-dependent affinity matrices that must be refreshed for every token configuration.

Data movement in attention creates memory access patterns distinct from fixed-stencil operations. Evaluating attention requires projecting and streaming query, key, and value tensors, then routing value vectors according to dynamically evaluated scores. In naive implementations, staging the intermediate \(\SequenceLength \times \SequenceLength\) score matrix in global HBM/DRAM turns score materialization into the dominant bandwidth bottleneck. While static convolutions benefit from deterministic cache lines and RNNs reuse weights across time, naive attention generates large, short-lived activation footprints that challenge standard on-chip cache capacities.

Checkpoint 6.4: Quadratic scaling intuition

Long-context scaling is shaped by the cost of attention. Verify your intuition:

Attention replaces the serialized hidden-state bottleneck of recurrent models with dynamic, constant-depth routing, but shifts the system burden to score arithmetic and memory bandwidth traffic. Built into a full architecture without recurrence, this primitive enables end-to-end parallel sequence processing on modern accelerators. The transformer architecture (section 6.7) builds directly on this foundation, establishing the dominant architecture for modern large-scale machine learning systems.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Why does scaled dot-product attention divide the query-key dot product \(\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{K}^T\) by \(\sqrt{d_{\text{head}}}\) prior to applying the softmax normalization function?

    1. To convert the matrix multiplication into a sparse graph lookup that reduces compute complexity from \(\mathcal{O}(S^2)\) to \(\mathcal{O}(S)\).
    2. Scaling limits the magnitude of attention scores as \(d_{\text{head}}\) grows, helping keep softmax from saturating too sharply; it does not remove quadratic score computation or guarantee universal overflow protection.
    3. To force the sum of all elements in the unnormalized query-key matrix to equal exactly 1.0 before applying softmax.
    4. To eliminate the need for Key weight matrices by making Query and Value representations mathematically identical.

  2. Consider a single transformer self-attention layer processing a sequence of length \(S = 4{,}096\) with \(N_{\text{heads}} = 12\) attention heads in FP16 precision (2 bytes per score). Calculate the memory required to store the materialized attention score matrices \((\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{K}^T)\) for this single layer, and explain why doubling the context length to \(S = 8{,}192\) creates a super-linear memory wall.

  3. True or False: IO-aware algorithms like FlashAttention reduce the computational complexity of dense self-attention from \(\mathcal{O}(S^2)\) down to \(\mathcal{O}(S)\) floating-point operations.

  4. In the scaled dot-product attention mechanism, the input sequence is projected into three distinct learned representations known as Queries, Keys, and ____, drawing a direct analogy to content-addressable retrieval systems.

  5. In an attention layer with sequence length \(S = 512\) and per-head feature dimension \(d_k = 64\), how many multiply-accumulate (MAC) operations are required to compute the query-key attention scores \((\mathbf{Q}\mathbf{K}^T)\) for a single attention head, excluding softmax normalization and value aggregation?

    1. 32,768 MACs, calculated as \(512 \times 64\).
    2. 262,144 MACs, calculated as \(512 \times 512\).
    3. 1,048,576 MACs, calculated as \(512 \times 512 \times 4\).
    4. 16,777,216 MACs (~16.8 million MACs), calculated as \(S \times S \times d_k = 512 \times 512 \times 64\).

See Answers →

6.7 Transformers: Parallel Sequence Processing

Attention provides the computational primitive of dynamic, content-dependent routing between positions, yet it was originally layered on top of recurrent architectures, inheriting their sequential bottleneck. The transformer architecture removes recurrence and makes attention the operation that mixes information between positions in every layer. This enables parallel computation across sequence positions during full-sequence processing while retaining dynamic connectivity. This architectural shift introduces distinct physical costs: quadratic attention-score cost over long inputs, a store of per-token state that grows while the model generates text, and memory-bandwidth pressure when it generates one token at a time.

Definition 6.6: Transformers

Transformers are neural network architectures that combine self-attention, feed-forward layers, residual connections, normalization, and positional information without recurrence, enabling parallel processing across sequence positions during training and prompt processing.

  1. Significance: Parallelism is the systems payoff. An RNN processing an \(\SequenceLength\)-token sequence executes \(\SequenceLength\) sequentially dependent steps. A transformer processes all positions of a sequence concurrently, mapping token interactions to large matrix multiplications (GEMMs) that maximize accelerator compute utilization. Dense self-attention retains the pairwise score cost (section 6.6.4.1).
  2. Distinction: Unlike attention used inside a recurrent backbone, which inherits the host architecture’s \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength)\) sequential path, transformers use attention as the primary mixing operation between positions, alternating it with position-wise feed-forward networks.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that transformers have “infinite context.” Two distinct memory footprints matter: the \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength^2)\) attention-score activation matrix when it is materialized over a long input, and the \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength)\) key-value (KV) cache that accumulates while the model generates text. At long sequence lengths, the KV cache footprint can rival or exceed the static model weights, turning KV cache management and memory-bandwidth efficiency into dominant engineering constraints.

6.7.1 Pattern processing needs

The recurrent sequence-to-sequence hybrids that first carried attention (Sutskever et al. 2014; Bahdanau et al. 2015) fixed the single-vector information bottleneck of section 6.5.5, yet step \(\TimeStepIndex\) still waited on step \(\TimeStepIndex-1\): a 1,000-token input still paid 1,000 ordered state updates no matter how many matrix units the accelerator offered. Dropping the recurrent loop so all 1,000 tokens can be processed at once places three pattern-processing demands on the replacement block. It must link any two positions within the same sequence in a single parallel pass; it must inject sequence order explicitly, because without step-by-step state updates an all-to-all operation cannot tell whether one word came before or after another; and it must apply nonlinear feature transformations within each position after routing information between them. The transformer architecture (Vaswani et al. 2017) meets those three requirements by pairing self-attention with positional encodings and position-wise feed-forward networks, trading the spatial parameter efficiency of CNNs and the bounded \(\mathcal{O}(1)\) step state of RNNs for unconstrained sequence parallelism during training and prompt processing.

Sutskever, Ilya, Oriol Vinyals, and Quoc V. Le. 2014. “Sequence to Sequence Learning with Neural Networks.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 27: 3104–12.

6.7.2 Algorithmic structure

To link tokens within a single sequence in one parallel pass, the transformer uses the self-attention projection introduced in section 6.6.2. Whereas encoder-decoder attention draws queries from a target sequence and keys and values from a separate source sequence, self-attention applies all three learned projection matrices (\(\QueryProjectionMatrix\), \(\KeyProjectionMatrix\), and \(\AttentionValuesProjectionMatrix\)) to the same input sequence \(\LayerInputMatrix\), as equation 11 writes in full: \[ \text{SelfAttention}(\LayerInputMatrix) = \text{softmax} \left(\frac{\LayerInputMatrix\QueryProjectionMatrix(\LayerInputMatrix\KeyProjectionMatrix)^T}{\sqrt{\AttentionHeadDimension}}\right)\LayerInputMatrix\AttentionValuesProjectionMatrix \tag{11}\]

Without positional information or an attention mask, matrix multiplication and row-wise softmax operate symmetrically across the token dimension, which makes self-attention permutation-equivariant. Shuffling the rows of \(\LayerInputMatrix\) merely shuffles the output rows identically, treating the sequence as an unordered set. Positional encodings, whether added as sinusoidal vectors or applied via rotary position embeddings (Su et al. 2024), inject sequence order and relative distance.

Su, Jianlin, Murtadha Ahmed, Yu Lu, Shengfeng Pan, Wen Bo, and Yunfeng Liu. 2024. “RoFormer: Enhanced Transformer with Rotary Position Embedding.” Neurocomputing 568: 127063. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neucom.2023.127063.

25 Attention scaling \((\sqrt{\AttentionHeadDimension})\): This normalization directly counteracts the linear growth in variance \((\AttentionHeadDimension)\) of the query-key dot product, preventing the softmax function from saturating where gradients would otherwise vanish. The systems consequence is most acute in mixed-precision training: when activations grow large, unscaled dot products can produce logits that overflow the 16-bit float range, destabilizing or halting learning entirely.

Conceptually, attention implements a differentiable content-addressable memory. Whereas conventional memory architectures retrieve stored values at explicit addresses, self-attention uses the query vector \(\QueryVector_i\) to score similarity against all candidate keys \(\AttentionKeyMatrix\in \mathbb{R}^{\SequenceLength\times \AttentionHeadDimension}\). The resulting softmax distribution generates a continuous interpolation over the value vectors \(\AttentionValuesMatrix\in \mathbb{R}^{\SequenceLength\times \AttentionHeadDimension}\), functioning as a differentiable lookup operation. The \(\sqrt{\AttentionHeadDimension}\) divisor keeps that lookup numerically well-conditioned via a variance argument: if query and key components are independent random variables with zero mean and unit variance, their inner product \(\QueryVector_i \cdot \KeyVector_j = \sum_{\HeadComponentIndex=1}^{\AttentionHeadDimension} \QueryComponent \KeyComponent\) has zero mean and variance \(\AttentionHeadDimension\), so dividing by \(\sqrt{\AttentionHeadDimension}\) restores unit variance and keeps softmax out of its saturated, near-zero-gradient region at large head dimensions.25

Transformers extend single-head self-attention by executing multiple attention operations in parallel through multi-head attention. Instead of computing a single set of attention weights over \(\HiddenDimension\) dimensions, the architecture projects queries, keys, and values into \(\AttentionHeadCount\) distinct lower-dimensional subspaces of dimension \(\AttentionHeadDimension = \HiddenDimension/ \AttentionHeadCount\). Each head can learn a different pattern. Their outputs are concatenated and projected through an output matrix \(\AttentionOutputProjectionMatrix\), as formalized in equation 12: \[ \text{MultiHead}(\QuerySourceMatrix, \KeySourceMatrix, \AttentionValuesSourceMatrix) = \text{Concat}(\text{head}_1, \ldots, \text{head}_{\AttentionHeadCount})\AttentionOutputProjectionMatrix \tag{12}\] where each attention head is computed as follows. In this multi-head notation, \(\QuerySourceMatrix\), \(\KeySourceMatrix\), and \(\AttentionValuesSourceMatrix\) denote the source representations before the head-specific projections; for self-attention, all three are \(\LayerInputMatrix\). The head matrices apply the projections once: \[ \text{head}_i = \text{Attention}(\QuerySourceMatrix\HeadQueryProjectionMatrix, \KeySourceMatrix\HeadKeyProjectionMatrix, \AttentionValuesSourceMatrix\HeadValuesProjectionMatrix) \]

Although individual attention heads often specialize in distinct syntactic or semantic patterns (Clark et al. 2019), adapting the effective connectivity graph to each input sequence, empirical analyses reveal substantial head redundancy and acute numerical sensitivity in reduced-precision formats. These hardware-relevant behaviors motivate structural pruning, low-rank factorization, sparse attention patterns, and specialized quantization strategies.

Clark, Kevin, Urvashi Khandelwal, Omer Levy, and Christopher D. Manning. 2019. “What Does BERT Look at? An Analysis of BERT’s Attention.” Proceedings of the 2019 ACL Workshop BlackboxNLP: Analyzing and Interpreting Neural Networks for NLP, 276–86. https://doi.org/10.18653/v1/w19-4828.

To build deep networks from these dynamic projections, the transformer places self-attention inside a repeating block (figure 12). Each block wraps multi-head attention and position-wise feed-forward sublayers with residual connections and layer normalization. Sinusoidal positional encodings combine with token embeddings at the base of both towers, feeding repeated multi-head attention and feed-forward stages before terminating in linear and softmax output heads. Because all positions can be processed in parallel rather than sequentially, the architecture trades recurrent state for large matrix operations that map well to accelerator training. A decoder-only model such as GPT-2 keeps only the right-hand tower of this figure, without the attention that reads an encoder’s output. Tokens enter as embedding vectors, pass through the stack of blocks, and leave as a probability distribution over the vocabulary for the next token. The residual paths and normalization layers in this stack are shared building blocks rather than transformer inventions; section 6.4.1 and section 6.4.2 explain why they make deep stacks trainable and what each costs the machine.

Language models built from this block are usually autoregressive: they produce text one token at a time and feed each new token back as input for the next. Given the prompt “The cat sat on,” a model predicts “the,” appends it, predicts “mat” from the longer context, and continues until it emits an end token. A causal mask keeps that process honest. It sets the attention scores for later positions to negative infinity before the softmax, so each position attends only to itself and earlier tokens, exactly the information that will exist when the model generates.

The same mask is what lets training run in parallel. During next-token training the whole target sequence is already known, so shifting it right by one position pairs every input token with its successor (figure 12), and the mask guarantees that position \(\TimeStepIndex\) cannot see later tokens. An accelerator therefore evaluates every position at once with dense matrix multiplications instead of stepping through a serial time loop as an RNN must.

Generation splits into two phases with different machine profiles, drawn in figure 13. Prefill processes the whole prompt in one pass: every prompt token is known, so the model evaluates them in parallel, as in training, and stores each token’s keys and values. Decode then produces the remaining tokens one per step. Each step feeds a single token through the model, so its projections shrink from matrix-matrix to matrix-vector products, and its attention reads the stored keys and values of every earlier token instead of recomputing them.
