That store is the key-value (KV) cache, and section 6.7.4.2 shows why decode, not prefill, usually sets a language model’s serving cost.

6.7.3 Computational mapping

In code, self-attention is three projections of the same input followed by the attention operator, and multi-head attention runs several smaller copies side by side before a final projection (listing 8). Writing both layers side by side shows how the multi-head variant partitions the hidden dimension across heads without changing the per-head attention kernel.

The attention listing accounts for information mixing across positions, but a complete block also transforms features within each position. An ordinary position-wise feed-forward network applies a dense projection from hidden width \(\HiddenDimension\) to an intermediate layer output width \(\LayerOutputWidth\), a nonlinear activation, and a second dense projection back to \(\HiddenDimension\). For a sequence of length \(\SequenceLength\), its tensor shapes follow \(\SequenceLength{\times}\HiddenDimension \to \SequenceLength{\times}\LayerOutputWidth \to \SequenceLength{\times}\HiddenDimension\). The same two weight matrices apply independently at every position, so the MLP accounting gives \(2\SequenceLength\HiddenDimension\LayerOutputWidth\) MACs and an intermediate tensor with \(\SequenceLength\LayerOutputWidth\) elements.

For one full-sequence block at batch one, with one standard multi-head self-attention sublayer and that two-layer feed-forward network, the query, key, value, and output projections together require \(4\SequenceLength\HiddenDimension^2\) MACs. The two attention products add \(2\SequenceLength^2\HiddenDimension\) MACs across all heads, whose widths sum to \(\HiddenDimension\). Adding the feed-forward cost completes the block’s matrix-product account. These counts omit biases, softmax, normalization, nonlinear activations, and residual additions, and assume dense evaluation of all position pairs. At fixed widths, projections and the feed-forward network grow linearly with sequence length, while the pairwise products grow quadratically. Which part dominates therefore depends on both the sequence length and the chosen widths, even before memory traffic is measured.

During autoregressive inference, generating output tokens step-by-step reintroduces sequential serialization. At batch one, projection matrix multiplications become matrix-vector operations with less weight reuse, making off-chip bandwidth a likely limit when weights exceed cache capacity, as quantified in section 6.7.4 via the GPT-2 XL lighthouse.

6.7.4 System implications

The quadratic bottleneck analyzed in section 6.6.4 manifests differently across execution regimes. Because full-sequence training and prompt prefill operate on known token sequences under causal masking, accelerators evaluate all positions concurrently using dense matrix multiplications. Autoregressive decoding, by contrast, must generate each token before predicting the next. That loss of token concurrency reduces weight reuse and can shift the primary performance limit from arithmetic throughput to memory bandwidth and state retrieval. Sequence length, batch size, and memory hierarchy limits determine whether that transition occurs.

Training and prefill

During training and prompt prefill, all token positions are processed in parallel, allowing accelerator Tensor Cores to achieve high floating-point utilization, but dense attention’s score computation grows as \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength^2)\) with sequence length \(\SequenceLength\). I/O-aware tiling such as FlashAttention (Dao et al. 2022) keeps the score matrix out of HBM (section 6.6.4.1), so the quadratic arithmetic, not its storage, is the wall that remains.

Dao, T., D. Y. Fu, S. Ermon, A. Rudra, and C. Ré. 2022. “FlashAttention: Fast and Memory-Efficient Exact Attention with IO-Awareness.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 35: 16344–59. https://doi.org/10.52202/068431-1189.

That wall arrives later than its name suggests. By the block account of section 6.7.3, with a feed-forward width four times the model width, a block’s projections and feed-forward network cost \(12\SequenceLength\HiddenDimension^2\) multiply-accumulates against \(2\SequenceLength^2\HiddenDimension\) for the attention products. Attention’s arithmetic therefore dominates only once the sequence passes about six times the model width, near 9,600 tokens for GPT-2 XL. Shorter training sequences are bound by the ordinary dense matrix multiplies, and the quadratic wall is a long-context phenomenon.

Expanding sequence length \(\SequenceLength\) challenges accelerator memory hierarchies because full-sequence self-attention cost grows with context length. Product context windows grew from 2K tokens to beyond 128K and into the 1M-token range within a few years (OpenAI 2023; Anthropic 2023; Google 2024), and the decoding discussion below sizes what that growth demands of memory. Longer context windows shift complexity from external retrieval and document chunking pipelines into accelerator memory capacity and bandwidth budgets. Exact tiling changes how much attention state is stored; sparse attention instead reduces the interactions computed (section 6.6.4.1).

OpenAI. 2023. New Models and Developer Products Announced at DevDay.
Anthropic. 2023. Introducing Claude 2.1.
Google. 2024. Our Next-Generation Model: Gemini 1.5.

Example 6.4: The quadratic wall
Scenario: BERT was pretrained with 512 learned position embeddings, so its deployments enforced a hard 512-token input limit (Devlin et al. 2019).

Diagnosis: Inputs beyond the pretrained positions are undefined for the model, however much memory is available. The limit itself was chosen partly because attention cost grows quadratically with sequence length. Extending from 512 to 4,096 tokens would multiply materialized score memory by 64×.

Systems lesson: Super-linear scaling turns algorithmic complexity into a hardware constraint that becomes fixed in the model itself once pretraining chooses it. No serving-time memory fix extends BERT past 512 positions, so its longer inputs must be truncated or split into windows. A model meant for long inputs must be pretrained with longer positions, and tiled or sparse attention is what keeps that training and its serving within the HBM activation budget.

Devlin, Jacob, Ming-Wei Chang, Kenton Lee, and Kristina Toutanova. 2019. “BERT: Pre-Training of Deep Bidirectional Transformers for Language Understanding.” Proceedings of the 2019 Conference of the North American Chapter of the Association for Computational Linguistics: Human Language Technologies, 4171–86. https://doi.org/10.18653/v1/n19-1423.

Autoregressive decoding

Autoregressive decoding generates one token at a time and often becomes memory-bandwidth bound at batch one (\(\BatchSize = 1\)) or small batch sizes when weights exceed on-chip memory. During prompt prefill or training, weight reuse across many token positions produces dense GEMMs with much higher arithmetic intensity. A large request batch (\(\BatchSize \gg 1\)) supplies many such positions, but prefill can obtain this reuse even for a single request with a long prompt.

During single-token generation (\(\BatchSize = 1\)), however, each projection layer collapses into a matrix-vector multiplication (GEMV). In the streamed-weight regime, the processor reads each projection weight from DRAM to multiply it by a single new token vector. This is the batch-one regime that equation 2 already derived for the MNIST layer at 0.5 FLOP/byte in FP32. Halving the weight width to 2 bytes in FP16, while still executing one multiply-accumulate operation (\(1\text{ MAC} = 2\text{ FLOPs}\)) per weight read, doubles that weight-only arithmetic intensity to exactly: \[\GemvArithmeticIntensity= \frac{2\text{ FLOPs}}{2\text{ bytes}} = 1.0\text{ FLOP/byte}\]

↩︎ The 1.0 FLOP/byte intensity of batch-one GEMV in 16-bit precision, and 0.5 FLOP/byte in FP32, is established in Optimizing inference throughput and latency.

Because modern accelerators require tens to hundreds of FLOPs per byte to reach peak compute throughput, running at \(1.0\text{ FLOP/byte}\) leaves compute cores idling for the vast majority of execution time, waiting for weights to arrive across the memory bus. To generate each token, the system must first stream every model parameter from off-chip memory into registers (for example, a 70B-parameter model streams 140 GB of FP16 weights per token, and GPT-2 XL makes that cost concrete below). It then executes the projection matrix-vector products at an arithmetic intensity capped at \(1.0\text{ FLOP/byte}\). Finally, it appends the new key and value vectors to the KV cache while streaming all historical keys and values from memory.

The KV cache26 introduced with prefill and decode grows linearly with sequence length (\(\mathcal{O}(\LayerCount \times 2 \times \AttentionHeadCount \times \SequenceLength\times \AttentionHeadDimension)\) per request, distinct from the \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength^2)\) attention score matrix during training). It stores the key and value vectors for all previous tokens to avoid recomputing them (Pope et al. 2023; Kwon et al. 2023). Keys and values from past tokens are retained because the newly generated token’s query (\(\QueryVector_{t}\)) must attend across all historical context (\(\KeyVector_1 \dots \KeyVector_{t-1}\) and \(\AttentionValuesVector_1 \dots \AttentionValuesVector_{t-1}\)) to compute its next representation. Prior queries (\(\QueryVector_1 \dots \QueryVector_{t-1}\)) are safely discarded because past tokens never attend forward to future tokens, while caching past keys and values avoids re-running the entire model forward pass over the full prompt at every generated token. For long contexts, this cache can become large (for example, 100+ GB), and each decoding step reads prior keys and values. The resulting bandwidth pressure depends on context length, batching, attention architecture, cache layout, and hardware.

26 KV cache memory scaling: For a 7-billion-parameter transformer in FP16, model weights consume ~14 GB; a single request’s cache requires 32 layers \(\times\) 2 (K,V) \(\times\) 32 heads \(\times\) 2,048 positions \(\times\) 128 dimensions \(\times\) 2 bytes, or about 1.07 GB. At 8 concurrent users, the cache is ~8.6 GB, a substantial addition to the weights. Because the cache grows linearly with context and concurrency, scaling throughput forces choices such as grouped-query attention (Ainslie et al. 2023), shorter contexts, or KV paging and offloading (Kwon et al. 2023). Paging and offloading preserve model outputs, whereas grouped-query attention changes the architecture and shorter contexts truncate historical context.

Ainslie, Joshua, James Lee-Thorp, Michiel de Jong, Yury Zemlyanskiy, Federico Lebron, and Sumit Sanghai. 2023. “GQA: Training Generalized Multi-Query Transformer Models from Multi-Head Checkpoints.” Proceedings of the 2023 Conference on Empirical Methods in Natural Language Processing, 4895–901. https://doi.org/10.18653/v1/2023.emnlp-main.298.
Kwon, Woosuk, Zhuohan Li, Siyuan Zhuang, Ying Sheng, Lianmin Zheng, Cody Hao Yu, Joseph Gonzalez, Hao Zhang, and Ion Stoica. 2023. “Efficient Memory Management for Large Language Model Serving with PagedAttention.” Proceedings of the 29th Symposium on Operating Systems Principles, 611–26. https://doi.org/10.1145/3600006.3613165.

↪ Budgeting KV cache memory across concurrent requests on a serving node is developed in Memory and KV cache.

For one model shape, the three memory regimes diverge sharply as context grows (figure 14). The weights do not grow with context at all, the KV cache grows linearly with every token a request keeps, and materialized attention scores grow with the square of the context. The cache overtakes the weights near 10,000 tokens, and one layer’s scores alone would fill an accelerator near 41,000 tokens, which is why serving systems size the cache per request and training kernels never materialize the scores.

GPT-2 (Radford et al. 2019) anchors the chapter’s bandwidth-bound language lighthouse. It is a decoder-only transformer, the same design as today’s large language models such as Llama, yet small enough that every weight and every byte of its decoding traffic can be counted by hand. Unlike BERT, an encoder-style transformer that reads its whole context in both directions, GPT-2 generates text one token at a time, so batch-one decoding reads every weight for every token it produces. Two differences from current decoders matter for the machine. GPT-2 gives every attention head its own keys and values, so it stores 307.2 KB of KV cache per token in FP16, while newer models share keys and values across groups of heads (Ainslie et al. 2023) and store several times less per parameter. Batching also amortizes weights across requests but not caches, because each sequence keeps its own, which is why serving at scale binds on KV bytes as well as weight bytes. At GPT-2 XL’s size, one decoding step reads its weights in about 0.9 ms on a data-center accelerator, a few times shorter than the time to launch the step’s many small kernels (ML Frameworks). Bandwidth therefore dominates cleanly only as models grow toward tens of billions of parameters.

Radford, Alec, Jeffrey Wu, Rewon Child, David Luan, Dario Amodei, and Ilya Sutskever. 2019. Language Models Are Unsupervised Multitask Learners. OpenAI.
Lighthouse 6.4: GPT-2 XL (bandwidth lighthouse)

Why it matters: GPT-2 XL exemplifies memory-bandwidth-bound batch-one decoding. In the weight-only model used here, each decoding step reads 6 GB of FP32 weights and performs matrix-vector operations. The resulting arithmetic intensity is about 0.5 FLOP/byte with FP32 weights or 1 FLOP/byte with FP16 weights. Batching can amortize this weight traffic, while KV-cache and activation traffic add to it. Table 5 summarizes the bandwidth lighthouse’s quantitative properties:

Across training and inference, transformers expose different opportunities for reuse. Full-sequence processing parallelizes across token positions while dense attention adds pairwise score cost, whereas small-batch autoregressive decoding stresses memory bandwidth when weights stream from off-chip memory.

Checkpoint 6.5: Prefill and decode

Yet dense, spatial, and sequence models do not cover the full spectrum of production workloads. Large-scale recommendation systems depart from standard arithmetic-dominated pipelines by coupling sparse embedding lookups (where memory capacity and irregular access distributions govern throughput) with downstream dense interaction layers. This hybrid matters at production scale: Meta reported that recommendation models accounted for most of its AI inference cycles (Gupta et al. 2020), even though such workloads receive less academic attention than language or vision models.

Gupta, Udit, Carole-Jean Wu, Xiaodong Wang, Maxim Naumov, Brandon Reagen, David Brooks, Bradford Cottel, et al. 2020. “The Architectural Implications of Facebook’s DNN-Based Personalized Recommendation.” 2020 IEEE International Symposium on High Performance Computer Architecture (HPCA), 488–501. https://doi.org/10.1109/HPCA47549.2020.00047.
Self-Check: Question

  1. Why does position-free unmasked self-attention require explicit positional information supplied by mechanisms such as sinusoidal signals, learned positional embeddings, or related position-encoding schemes?

    1. Because matrix multiplication hardware cannot process tensors without fixed static padding across all dimensions.
    2. Because layer normalization removes the mean and variance of token vectors, destroying word identity.
    3. Because without positional information, unmasked self-attention is permutation equivariant: permuting the input rows permutes the output rows in the same way, so the layer by itself does not encode order.
    4. Because positional encodings reduce the computational complexity of the attention matrix from \(\mathcal{O}(S^2)\) to \(\mathcal{O}(S)\).

  2. Under a weight-only memory model in FP16 precision, an autoregressive language model generates 1 token per forward pass at batch size 1, performing approximately 2 FLOPs per parameter while streaming the entire weight matrix from High Bandwidth Memory (HBM). Calculate the theoretical arithmetic intensity of this decoding step and explain why it causes accelerator matrix units (Tensor Cores) to remain severely underutilized.

  3. Order the sub-layer operations executed within a single standard Transformer encoder block during a forward pass:

  1. Project input activations into Query, Key, and Value tensors via linear weight matrices
  2. Compute multi-head scaled dot-product self-attention across all sequence positions
  3. Apply residual skip connection addition and layer normalization to the attention output
  4. Pass normalized representations through a position-wise two-layer feed-forward network (MLP)
  5. Apply residual skip connection addition and layer normalization to the feed-forward output

  1. A production serving system deploys a 32-layer transformer with 32 attention heads, head dimension \(d_{\text{head}} = 128\), and context length \(S = 2{,}048\) in FP16 precision (2 bytes per value). Calculate the memory footprint of the Key-Value (KV) cache for a single user request, and explain why KV-cache memory can surpass model weight memory under high concurrent batching.

  2. True or False: During autoregressive language model inference, single-token generation at batch size 1 achieves near-peak GPU floating-point throughput (TFLOP/s) because the matrix-vector multiplication is highly optimized.

  3. In a Multi-Head Attention layer with model dimension \(d_{\text{model}} = 768\) and \(N_{\text{heads}} = 12\) heads, what is the per-head dimension \(d_k\), and how does multi-head projection affect total computational FLOP complexity compared to a single attention head operating on the full 768 dimensions?

    1. The per-head dimension is \(d_k = 768\), increasing total projection FLOPs by \(12\times\) compared to a single head.
    2. The per-head dimension is \(d_k = 768 / 12 = 64\); running 12 heads of dimension 64 has the exact same total projection and score FLOP complexity as a single head of dimension 768, while enabling the model to jointly attend to information from 12 distinct representation subspaces.
    3. The per-head dimension is \(d_k = 12\), reducing total computational complexity by \(64\times\).
    4. Multi-head attention eliminates the output projection matrix \(\mathbf{W}^O\), halving layer parameter count.

6.8 Sparse Architectures: Recommendation Systems

Coupling sparse lookups with dense interaction layers is the defining pattern of industrial recommendation engines. When a user opens a streaming service or e-commerce platform, the system must select a handful of recommendations from a catalog of millions within an end-to-end budget of tens of milliseconds. Meeting that target requires mapping both users and items to dense vectors in a shared embedding space and scoring their interactions at scale, which exposes a regime governed first by memory capacity. A data-center accelerator engineered for dense matrix multiplication, providing hundreds of teraflops of arithmetic throughput alongside 80 GB of HBM, cannot hold terabytes of user and item embeddings locally. Its sparse stage gathers rows from those tables, placing different demands on the machine than the batched convolutions of section 6.3 or the streamed-weight decoding of section 6.7.

6.8.1 Pattern processing needs

A recommendation model must process user IDs across billions of accounts and item IDs across millions of products, yet raw integer IDs carry no inherent distance. User 1042 is no closer to user 1043 than to user 9,000,000, so a dense network cannot infer relationships from the numbers themselves. A single request also carries many such categorical features at once (the items a user recently viewed, the categories they follow), so one prediction can require dozens of separate ID lookups.

Embeddings solve the representation problem by mapping each discrete ID to a dense vector called an embedding27 (Mikolov et al. 2013). The vectors are ordinary learned parameters. Each row of an embedding table is a weight vector that gradient descent updates whenever its ID appears in a training example, so users and items with similar behavior end up near one another. The dot product of a user vector with an item vector becomes a learned score for how well they match. The system cost is that every lookup becomes an irregular read into a table spanning tens or hundreds of gigabytes. Recommendation models therefore encounter a capacity wall and a memory-bandwidth bottleneck before the first dense feedforward layer executes.

27 Embedding: Deriving from the mathematical embedding of one space into another, neural embeddings map discrete tokens (such as user IDs or vocabulary words) into continuous vector spaces where geometric proximity captures semantic similarity. Word2vec popularized neural word embeddings in 2013. For systems, embedding tables create a distinctive memory access pattern: each lookup is an irregular read into a potentially terabyte-scale table, producing a sparse, bandwidth-bound workload that makes DLRM different from compute-bound architectures like ResNet.

Mikolov, Tomas, Kai Chen, Greg Corrado, and Jeffrey Dean. 2013. “Efficient Estimation of Word Representations in Vector Space.” ICLR abs/1301.3781.

6.8.2 Algorithmic structure

The DLRM architecture (Naumov et al. 2019) organizes this computation into a four-stage pipeline split between dense and sparse paths. Continuous features such as user age or time of day first flow through a bottom MLP, a compute-intensive but memory-light stage dominated by dense matrix multiplications. The bottom MLP projects the raw continuous inputs into a single dense vector with the same dimension as each embedding table row (such as 128 elements), placing continuous and categorical features in a common vector space. In parallel, categorical IDs index into dedicated embedding tables, which is where the memory capacity wall appears.

For example, an embedding table for one billion users with 128-dimensional vectors requires \(10^9 \times 128 \times 4\) bytes \(\approx\) 512 GB of memory. This stage is memory-intensive but compute-light because each lookup is a memory copy with zero arithmetic reuse. Because the bottom MLP output and every gathered embedding row now share the same vector length, the interaction layer can combine the dense activations from the bottom MLP with the gathered sparse embedding vectors through pairwise dot products that capture user-item correlations. Finally, a top MLP processes the concatenated interaction representations through feedforward layers to predict a target probability such as click-through rate. Each of the four stages stresses a different part of the machine (figure 15), and section 6.8.3 quantifies the capacity-bound profile that results.

6.8.3 Computational mapping and system implications

DLRM’s computational mapping splits into two regimes that stress different hardware subsystems. The dense MLPs execute standard GEMM operations, identical to the MLP computational mapping discussed in section 6.2.4 and handled efficiently by Tensor Cores. The sparse embedding lookups, however, are qualitatively different. They are index-based memory gathers that copy parameter data with zero arithmetic reuse. An isolated lookup is bound by memory access latency. When a batch executes hundreds of lookups across features and samples, however, aggregate throughput becomes memory-bandwidth bound as concurrent requests saturate the memory bus. This operational throughput limit is distinct from the capacity constraint: total table size dictates whether parameters fit in accelerator memory or must spill across PCIe to host DRAM, whereas available bandwidth governs how fast gathered rows arrive. Within each selected row, an embedding vector is contiguous (for instance, 128 FP32 elements spanning 512 bytes), allowing the downstream pooling loop to consume every transferred byte. Across rows, however, data-dependent categorical IDs lack predictable spatial locality, defeating hardware prefetchers between row accesses. Access popularity remains heavily skewed: a small set of hot rows (popular items, frequent users) shows strong temporal locality in on-chip caches, while the long tail of rarely touched rows misses to main memory. Listing 9 writes this sparse stage as a loop nest parallel to those of the other families.

DLRM (Naumov et al. 2019), which Meta open-sourced as a reference for this hybrid architecture, anchors the chapter’s recommendation lighthouse. Its combination of gigabyte-scale embedding tables and small multilayer perceptrons serves as the standard benchmark for memory capacity and irregular sparse access in the data center.

Naumov, Maxim, Dheevatsa Mudigere, Hao-Jun Michael Shi, Jianyu Huang, Narayanan Sundaraman, Jongsoo Park, Xiaodong Wang, et al. 2019. “Deep Learning Recommendation Model for Personalization and Recommendation Systems.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1906.00091.

↪ The share of DLRM serving latency spent on embedding lookups is developed in Stage-by-stage latency breakdown.

Lighthouse 6.5: DLRM (recommendation lighthouse)

Why it matters: DLRM exemplifies memory-capacity-bound workloads. Its large embedding tables often exceed the memory of a single accelerator or server, so the system must decide where those tables live before it can optimize arithmetic throughput. The interaction layer then gathers selected embedding vectors and combines them with dense features, making capacity and irregular data movement the dominant constraints. This capacity-first decision differs from the batched convolution and streamed-weight decoding regimes examined here, requiring different hardware and deployment choices. Table 6 summarizes the recommendation lighthouse’s quantitative properties.

Once embedding tables exceed one device, capacity, not arithmetic, becomes the primary systems constraint. While a ResNet-50 (102.2 MB) fits on a single accelerator and a dense language model like GPT-3 (Brown et al. 2020) (350 GB) fits within a single multi-GPU node, production recommendation models reach terabytes of embedding parameters. In iron law terms, capacity acts as a feasibility constraint that comes before either execution term. It decides where the tables live, and therefore which bandwidth the data term \(\DataVolume/\Bandwidth\) must use: HBM when the tables fit, host DRAM over PCIe or a peer accelerator over NVLink when they do not.

Brown, Tom B., Benjamin Mann, Nick Ryder, Melanie Subbiah, Jared Kaplan, Prafulla Dhariwal, Arvind Neelakantan, et al. 2020. “Language Models Are Few-Shot Learners.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 33: 1877–901. https://doi.org/10.48550/arxiv.2005.14165.

The architecture therefore breaks the single-device assumption that holds for smaller dense models. Systems designers have three ways to resolve the capacity wall. They can shrink the tables through compression, hashing, or pruning, at the risk of losing information on long-tail users and items. They can move the tables to host DRAM or external key-value stores, at the cost of slower remote lookups over PCIe or Ethernet. They can partition the tables across multiple accelerators so that no single device stores the full model, trading local memory pressure for cross-device communication. Architecturally, DLRM turns recommendation into a capacity-management challenge before it becomes a compute-optimization task. Training-time model partitioning and hardware-accelerated interconnects address these distributed layouts, and the baseline sizing calculation begins with a single table.

One embedding table fits an 80 GB A100; a second breaches the capacity wall.

Napkin Math 6.2: The capacity wall
Problem: Consider a recommendation system for a store with 100M items using an embedding size of 128. How much memory does the item table alone require?

Math:

  1. Table entries: 100M items.
  2. Vector size: 128 elements.
  3. Precision: FP32 (4 bytes per element).
  4. Table size: 100M items \(\times\) 128 \(\times\) 4 bytes ≈ 51.2 GB.
  5. Capacity share: 51.2 GB \(\div\) 80 GB = 64 percent of device capacity.
  6. Two-table check: Two tables at 64 percent each exceed 100 percent of device capacity.

Systems insight: A single embedding table for one feature (items) already consumes 64 percent of an 80 GB A100 GPU. Adding a user table of the same size means the embedding tables no longer fit on a single 80 GB accelerator, motivating table compression, off-device memory, or partitioning across devices. DLRM is capacity bound: the primary systems question is not how many FLOP/s the accelerator can deliver, but where the embedding state can physically reside.

Once the tables no longer fit on one device, sparse lookups cross memory and interconnect boundaries. A request may need rows owned by several accelerators, forcing the device executing the downstream MLP to wait while the system gathers them across PCIe or NVLink. Within a multi-accelerator server, gathering rows distributed across multiple GPUs turns memory routing into an interconnect bandwidth bottleneck, because every accelerator may need rows held by every other, an all-to-all exchange. The architectural consequence is that a recommendation model’s memory layout directly determines its cross-accelerator data-transfer traffic, creating coordination overhead for both training and serving.

Sharded tables can turn lookups into all-to-all accelerator traffic.

↪ Partitioning a model that exceeds one device’s memory across accelerators, and the communication that partition boundaries add, is developed in Model parallelism.

Checkpoint 6.6: DLRM and capacity-bound lookups

Recommendation systems stress a different part of the machine than CNNs or transformers.

With DLRM, the roster of five families is complete, and each has been analyzed on its own terms: the pattern it targets, the computation it performs, the mapping it induces, and the bottleneck it exposes. A deployment rarely gets to examine one family in isolation. The same accelerator that serves a vision model today may serve a language model or a recommender tomorrow, and the architecture decides which of its resources gives out first.

Self-Check: Question

  1. In the Deep Learning Recommendation Model (DLRM) architecture, what is the primary computational role of the Interaction Layer?

    1. It applies 2D convolutions over user and item IDs to extract hierarchical spatial features.
    2. It normalizes categorical IDs across the batch using running mean and variance statistics.
    3. It performs autoregressive token decoding to predict the next search query.
    4. It computes pairwise dot products between the dense feature representations from the Bottom MLP and the sparse embedding vectors gathered from categorical tables to capture explicit feature interactions.

  2. Explain why a large-table recommendation scenario becomes memory-capacity-bound rather than compute-bound when the model’s combined embedding tables exceed the fast memory of a single accelerator, and why the standard execution form of the Iron Law of ML Systems (\(T_{\text{exec}} = D_{\text{vol}}/\text{BW} + O/(R_{\text{peak}} \cdot \eta_{\text{hw}}) + L_{\text{lat}}\)) cannot directly determine whether that model can be deployed on one accelerator.

  3. Order the four primary computational stages executed during an end-to-end inference pass in a DLRM recommendation model:

  1. Process continuous numerical features through the dense Bottom MLP to produce a dense representation
  2. Look up sparse categorical IDs across embedding tables to gather discrete embedding vectors
  3. Compute pairwise dot products (interactions) between the Bottom MLP output and all gathered embedding vectors
  4. Concatenate interaction dot products with Bottom MLP features and pass through the Top MLP to predict click-through probability

  1. An e-commerce recommendation system maintains an item embedding table with 100 million items (\(10^8\)) and a user embedding table with 1 billion users (\(10^9\)), each using 128-dimensional FP32 vectors (4 bytes per parameter). Calculate the memory footprint of each table, verify why they cannot fit on a single 80 GB A100 GPU, and describe two systems strategies to handle this capacity wall.

  2. Why do sparse embedding table lookups in recommendation workloads resist standard hardware caching and memory prefetching mechanisms that accelerate CNNs and MLPs?

    1. Because each incoming request queries arbitrary, non-contiguous row indices determined by sparse user and item IDs, producing irregular gathers with weak spatial locality, although popularity skew can still create temporal reuse for hot rows.
    2. Because embedding tables are permanently encrypted in DRAM, preventing hardware prefetchers from reading address buses.
    3. Because embedding lookups require performing high-order tensor contractions that stall CPU prefetch queues.
    4. Because recommendation systems execute only on storage-class memory where hardware caching is disabled by operating system kernels.

See Answers →

6.9 Comparing and Choosing Architectures

The three requests that opened this chapter can now be costed in numbers. A single ResNet-50 frame performs about 8.2 GFLOP of arithmetic against 102.2 MB of FP32 weights, and each convolutional filter is reused across its feature map, creating opportunities to keep arithmetic units busy. A single GPT-2 token at batch size one performs about 3 GFLOP/token against 3 GB of FP16 weights. Streaming those weights once gives a weight-only arithmetic intensity of roughly 1 FLOP/byte, making bandwidth a likely limit. A DLRM request gathers a handful of embedding rows from tables totaling 100 GB, more than the accelerator’s 80 GB of memory, so before any question of speed arises the system must decide where those tables live.

The silicon is identical in all three cases, but the demands differ. Convolutional reuse favors arithmetic throughput, streamed token projections stress bandwidth, and oversized embedding tables impose a capacity constraint that decides which bandwidth their lookups pay. The iron law (Iron Law of ML Systems) then predicts which term binds, given the execution conditions and machine balance.

Each of those costs comes from a counting rule derived in its family’s section. Table 7 collects the rules in exact form, so that a new model can be estimated on paper in the same order: count the weights, count the multiply-accumulates, count the bytes that must move or stay resident, then compare those counts with the machine.

6.9.1 Arithmetic intensity across workloads

Each family section measured reuse in its own terms: filters reused across a feature map, weights streamed once per decoded token, rows gathered from a table. Arithmetic intensity, the FLOP/byte ratio set against a machine’s balance, puts them on one scale and shows whether each workload is likely to be compute or memory bound.

↩︎ Arithmetic intensity and the system balance it is compared against are established in System Balance and Hardware.

The calculation here is a weight-only proxy rather than a complete measurement of main-memory traffic. It divides floating-point work by FP32 model-weight bytes, omitting activation, intermediate, and KV-cache traffic. Batching can amortize weight traffic across examples or tokens, while additional state movement can lower realized intensity. Operation cost reference gives the per-operation FLOP and parameter formulas used in the proxy. Under these assumptions ResNet-50’s proxy exceeds GPT-2 XL’s by roughly 160×, the widest gap among the lighthouses in table 8.

The intensity proxy is one input to architecture selection. Deployment constraints extend beyond the FLOP-to-byte ratio, and batch size, precision, sequence length, and target hardware decide whether the proxy predicts the deployed bottleneck. Table 8 sets the proxy beside parameter footprint, inference cost, and the governing hardware constraint for all five canonical workloads: compute throughput, memory bandwidth, memory capacity, latency, and power. For compute- and bandwidth-bound workloads, the iron law directly maps the primary bottleneck to operations (\(\Operations\)) or data movement (\(\DataVolume\)). For capacity-, latency-, and power-constrained workloads, physical boundaries dictate where and within what budget those operations must execute, determining which optimization strategies prove effective.

Architecture selection is ultimately an engineering trade-off between math \((\Operations)\) and memory movement \((\DataVolume)\). The mechanisms behind the intensities in table 8 explain why each lighthouse sits where it does on the intensity spectrum. ResNet-50 earns its high intensity because convolutional layers reuse each weight many times across the spatial dimensions of an image (individual deeper ResNet layers reach 100–200+ FLOP/byte). Arithmetic throughput becomes the limit when effective intensity exceeds the target machine’s compute-to-bandwidth balance. GPT-2 sits at the opposite extreme during batch-one decoding with streamed weights: each projection loads weights for one token’s matrix-vector product, making bandwidth a likely limit. MobileNet lands between the two at the whole-model level, with individual depthwise layers falling lower once activation traffic is included. Depthwise separable convolutions reduce total \(\Operations\) but move more data relative to that work, which fits mobile hardware well yet often “starves” high-end GPUs optimized for dense math.

Checkpoint 6.7: Arithmetic intensity and architecture

Match the architectural choice to its systems implication:

Comparing this spectrum with the target machine’s balance helps determine whether faster arithmetic or faster memory would improve performance. The roofline model (Williams et al. 2009) plots achievable throughput against arithmetic intensity to show where a workload turns from memory bound to compute bound. This chapter needs only the comparison introduced with system balance, a workload’s intensity set against the machine’s ratio of peak compute to memory bandwidth. The full construction, including the ridge point of an H100 and how the same operation falls on either side of it, is collected for reference in A concrete example: The H100 analysis.

Williams, Samuel, Andrew Waterman, and David Patterson. 2009. “Roofline: An Insightful Visual Performance Model for Multicore Architectures.” Communications of the ACM 52 (4): 65–76. https://doi.org/10.1145/1498765.1498785.

↪ The roofline model’s analytical treatment, from the memory-bound slope to the ridge point, is developed in Performance Bounds.

Intensity is one column of a fuller profile. Persistent state, per-request state, the axis along which the work parallelizes, and the iron-law term that binds complete the comparison.

6.9.2 Workload profiles

An architecture’s bottleneck depends on how a request exercises its state and parallelism as much as on its layer equations, which is why the transformer occupies two rows in table 9: prompt prefill and autoregressive decode reuse the exact same weights in different regimes (section 6.7.4.2). Each row of the table pairs a family in a representative execution regime with the workload archetypes of Workload archetypes. The compute-bound rows assume sufficient reuse to exceed the machine’s balance, the bandwidth-bound rows assume streamed operands, and the capacity-bound row assumes oversized tables. Reading across the columns follows a request through the machine: persistent state, per-request state, arithmetic intensity, parallel axes, and the likely limiting term under those assumptions.

Table 9 states memory as growth rates; for the lighthouses the bytes are concrete (table 10). Deployment usually stores weights in a narrow format, so INT8 cuts every row by four. Training moves the other way. Each FP32 weight also carries a gradient and two optimizer moments, so the parameter state alone is four times the FP32 weights before any activation is saved (System cost of optimizer state itemizes this 16 bytes per parameter). A model that serves from one accelerator may not train on it.

The parameter and activation columns of table 9 decide how data reaches the arithmetic units, and the pattern of access matters as much as the volume. Three patterns cover the table. Sequential access, the streaming of contiguous weight rows in an MLP layer or a transformer projection, lets DRAM deliver long bursts and lets hardware prefetchers run ahead, so it approaches the interface’s peak bandwidth. Strided access, which a convolution produces when its window steps across a layout that does not keep the window contiguous, fetches whole cache lines to use a fraction of each. The im2col lowering (section 6.3.3) moves that gather into a separate copy pass so the matrix multiply itself reads sequentially, at the cost of up to \(\KernelSize^2\)-fold duplication (because overlapping sliding windows copy the same input pixels into multiple rows). Random access characterizes the DLRM row. Sparse IDs index arbitrary rows across multi-gigabyte tables. While each selected embedding vector is contiguous and fully consumed by pooling, row selection lacks predictable spatial locality between rows, which defeats hardware prefetchers and leaves sparse lookups exposed to memory latency unless many concurrent requests are in flight. Achieved bandwidth depends on vector width, alignment, and request concurrency, which is why caching popular rows is critical to reduce off-chip traffic. Attention is not random access despite its content-dependent weights, because its queries, keys, and values sit in contiguous tensors that tiled kernels stream through on-chip SRAM.

Whether an access incurs DRAM traffic at all depends on the working set, the data the execution units need at once. Small layers, such as the \(784{\times}128\) reference layer at 401.4 KB in FP32, fit in on-chip SRAM. Production-width layers of tens of megabytes do not, which is why unbatched MLP inference and transformer decode stream their weights from off-chip memory on every request. Accelerators answer both ends of the table with memory hierarchy rather than arithmetic. HBM stacks DRAM dies beside the processor to supply the streaming rows at more than an order of magnitude above standard DDR channels. Multi-megabyte on-chip SRAM, often an explicitly managed scratchpad, lets compilers stage tiles so that reuse happens on chip. The intensity column measures how much a family can profit from that staging, and it doubles as an energy column.

Moving a 32-bit value from DRAM costs far more energy than one FP32 multiply.

Systems Perspective 6.2: Reuse sets each family's energy cost
Moving a 32-bit value from DRAM costs far more energy than one arithmetic operation on it (Energy Cost of Moving vs. Computing on a 32-Bit Value in The feeding problem). Against the full multiply-accumulate that a dense layer performs, the gap is about 139×, the basis used for the MLP and RNN above. How much of that cost each family pays depends on reuse, so low-intensity rows are energy-dominated by DRAM traffic. An unbatched MLP or a decode step loads each weight once per request and spends most of its energy moving it. A CNN amortizes each filter load over \(\FeatureMapHeight\times \FeatureMapWidth\) applications, so the locality that makes it compute bound also makes it energy efficient, unless a fully materialized im2col buffer inflates temporary traffic by up to \(\KernelSize^2\). An RNN keeps its compact state in SRAM but idles execution units across serial steps, which raises energy per token. Tiled attention keeps score blocks on chip, so its energy tracks matrix multiplication rather than DRAM round-trips.

The parallel-axis column determines how data must move among processing elements once the work is spread out. Four data movement patterns (broadcast, scatter, gather, and reduction; figure 16) govern how tensors travel among processing elements, whether those are units on one chip or several accelerators in one server. Batch parallelism in MLPs, CNNs, and prefill broadcasts stationary weight tiles to many units, each working on a different example or position, and compilers tile loops to reuse each broadcast value as often as possible. Spatial and model partitioning scatter a tensor across tiles or devices. Mixture-of-experts models, which route each token to a few of many feedforward subnetworks, make the scatter input-dependent, so a popular expert can overload one accelerator while others idle. DLRM gathers embedding rows from wherever the sharded tables live, and each attention query gathers keys and values from every position in its sequence. Reductions combine values through sums or maxima (softmax denominators, normalization statistics, and the summing of gradients across accelerators that each trained on a different slice of the batch). Tree-structured reduction hardware finishes a reduction over \(\ReductionInputCount\) values in \(\mathcal{O}(\log \ReductionInputCount)\) stages rather than \(\mathcal{O}(\ReductionInputCount)\). The rows with a serial axis, RNNs and decode, are the rows these collectives cannot rescue, because no amount of broadcast or reduction bandwidth shortens a dependency chain.

The last column applies the iron law under each row’s execution assumptions. When effective intensity exceeds the machine’s balance and parallel work is sufficient (batched CNNs, prefill), the compute term \(\Operations/(\PeakRate\,\EfficiencyHw)\) binds. Below that balance (streamed small-batch MLPs, decode), the data term \(\DataVolume/\Bandwidth\) binds, favoring fewer transferred bytes, more reuse, or higher bandwidth. RNNs add a serial chain of state updates: faster arithmetic, memory, and reduced launch overhead \((\Latency)\) can shorten each step without removing the dependency. DLRM adds a constraint that sits in front of the equation. Capacity decides where the tables live and thus which bandwidth the data term pays. ResNet-50, GPT-2, and DLRM isolate these representative regimes. MobileNetV2 and KWS share the CNN row, with depthwise layers pulling their intensity down; they stand for latency and power, deployment budgets that bound the whole execution rather than one term. No lighthouse occupies the RNN row, whose serial chain the recurrent family section analyzes directly.

6.9.3 Shared building blocks

The rows of table 9 differ, but the operators inside them repeat. Each block was taught with the family that first needed it: dense projections with MLPs, skip connections and normalization with ResNet-50 (section 6.4.1 and section 6.4.2), and gating with recurrent networks (section 6.5.3). Table 11 connects each building block to a representative context and to other architectures that use it. Each block brings its hardware cost with it, so an optimization for residual additions or normalization kernels can transfer across families.

Dense projections

General matrix multiplication is the one primitive every row of the profile shares. It is the feedforward layer inside every transformer block, the \(1{\times}1\) pointwise convolution in MobileNets, the input and recurrent projections inside every RNN cell, and the bottom and top MLPs of DLRM. Convolution reaches the same path through the im2col patch unfolding of figure 5, and dedicated matrix engines, such as GPU Tensor Cores and the systolic arrays of TPUs, exist because this one primitive dominates the operation count of nearly every workload. Its hardware cost depends on shape rather than on the family that invokes it. Batching therefore acts on every family that reaches this path as it acts on the dense layer measured in Implementation and optimization considerations, converting a matrix-vector product into a matrix-matrix product with greater reuse. The higher intensity can cross the target machine’s balance point, which is why batch size changes the modeled regime of three rows of the table.

Dense connectivity supplies a common cost baseline, but each family changes a different part of the workload. Convolution shares filters across spatial positions; attention combines dense projections with pairwise interactions; and embedding lookup replaces an arithmetic transformation with an indexed read. Their parameter storage, operation counts, and transferred bytes must therefore be compared separately, as in table 9. Dense projections remain inside all these architectures, so improvements to their execution can transfer between families.

Gating, routing, and the transformer block

Gating generalizes beyond recurrence as the basic mechanism for data-dependent routing. Highway Networks (Srivastava et al. 2015) used gates to interpolate between a transformation and an identity bypass, a gated precursor of the residual shortcut. Encoder-decoder attention (Bahdanau et al. 2015) computes a dynamic routing distribution over input representations, and in transformers the softmax attention weights act as data-dependent routing coefficients that decide how features aggregate across tokens. Mixture-of-experts architectures extend the idea to dispatch each token to a subset of feedforward experts (Jacobs et al. 1991; Shazeer et al. 2017), which separates the weights a token reads, and so its FLOPs and bytes, from the model’s total capacity. The hardware cost of gating is irregularity. Dense attention keeps static tensor shapes even though its weights vary per token, but sparse attention, expert routing, and dynamic graph execution introduce data-dependent control flow and runtime tensor sizes that defeat static memory allocation and balanced work dispatch.

Srivastava, Rupesh Kumar, Klaus Greff, and Jürgen Schmidhuber. 2015. “Highway Networks.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1505.00387.
Bahdanau, Dzmitry, Kyunghyun Cho, and Yoshua Bengio. 2015. “Neural Machine Translation by Jointly Learning to Align and Translate.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Jacobs, Robert A., Michael I. Jordan, Steven J. Nowlan, and Geoffrey E. Hinton. 1991. “Adaptive Mixtures of Local Experts.” Neural Computation 3 (1): 79–87. https://doi.org/10.1162/neco.1991.3.1.79.
Shazeer, Noam, Azalia Mirhoseini, Krzysztof Maziarz, Andy Davis, Quoc Le, Geoffrey Hinton, and Jeff Dean. 2017. “Outrageously Large Neural Networks: The Sparsely-Gated Mixture-of-Experts Layer.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

↪ The irregular memory access that unstructured sparsity, embedding lookups, and dynamic routing impose on hardware is developed in Irregular memory access.

28 Vision transformers (ViTs): Google’s 2020 ViT paper split \(224{\times}224\) images into \(16{\times}16\) patches (196 “tokens”) and applied standard transformer attention. ViTs replace CNN’s local convolutions with \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength^2)\) global attention over patch tokens. In the original study, large-scale pretraining improved ViT’s competitiveness, illustrating how data and compute can compensate for weaker spatial inductive bias (Dosovitskiy et al. 2021).

Dosovitskiy, Alexey, Lucas Beyer, Alexander Kolesnikov, Dirk Weissenborn, Xiaohua Zhai, Thomas Unterthiner, Mostafa Dehghani, et al. 2021. “An Image Is Worth 16x16 Words: Transformers for Image Recognition at Scale.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

A transformer block is where all four building blocks meet. In the encoder tower of figure 12, residual paths bypass each multi-head attention and feed-forward sub-layer to preserve gradient flow through deep stacks, dense GEMM projections carry the attention and feedforward transformations, LayerNorm or RMSNorm stabilizes activations around each sub-layer, and softmax attention weights gate information across positions. What the transformer changed was the dependency structure. Replacing recurrence with all-to-all attention cut the sequential depth for tokens to interact from \(\mathcal{O}(\SequenceLength)\) steps to \(\mathcal{O}(1)\), which let the other three blocks run at full width on parallel hardware. Vision Transformers28 carry the same block set to images (Dosovitskiy et al. 2021).

This portability separates optimizations that transfer from those that do not. GEMM tiling and mixed-precision arithmetic help every row of table 9, residual memory management helps every deep network, and normalization kernel fusion helps CNNs and transformers alike. Attention-specific kernels remain tied to attention’s memory pattern, but even they are built from the same GEMM and memory-access primitives. The profile and the building blocks describe what each family costs; choosing among families for a particular deployment requires turning those costs into a procedure.

6.9.4 Architecture selection framework

Turning those family profiles into a selection procedure starts with a hard systems truth: deployment constraints eliminate architectures that look competitive on unconstrained accuracy benchmarks, and a structural mismatch chosen here cannot be rescued later by compiler optimization or kernel tuning. A battery-powered edge sensor bounded by milliwatts of thermal envelope and a data-center ranking service bounded by tens of milliseconds of tail latency rule out opposite rows of table 9 before training ever begins. Engineering teams nonetheless often default to architectures by popularity, deploying over-parameterized transformers where a compact convolutional network would suffice or selecting models whose state exceeds on-device memory. In D·A·M terms, architecture selection chooses the algorithm (\(\text{A}\)) that bridges the structure of the data (\(\text{D}\)) to the budgets of the machine (\(\text{M}\)): memory capacity, memory bandwidth, compute throughput, and thermal envelope.

Architecture selection turns on the algorithm axis, bridging data to machine.

Selection begins with data-to-architecture mapping, matching the symmetries of the input to an operator family. The useful strength of an inductive bias depends on the task (section 6.1). CNNs impose local connectivity, weight sharing, and translation equivariance, reducing parameter volume and exposing reuse for grid-like data. RNNs impose temporal recurrence, bounding inference state across sequence lengths at the cost of serialization. Transformers use content-based attention, gaining flexible interactions while paying for dense quadratic score computation. MLPs impose no specific spatial or temporal connectivity pattern, leaving those relationships to be learned. Ordered by the strength of their assumptions, the families therefore run from CNNs through RNNs and transformers to MLPs; the margin figure beside the inductive-bias definition draws the same ordering for CNNs, transformers, and MLPs. The stronger the prior, the fewer samples and the less computation a matching task needs, and the more a mismatched task loses, because a strong prior can exclude useful solutions as easily as it excludes useless ones. Model selection must test both predictive fit and the resulting machine costs, and the history of the Netflix Prize shows what happens when only the first test is run.

War Story 6.1: The ensemble Netflix did not ship (2009)
Context: Netflix awarded its $1M Grand Prize in 2009 for a 10 percent improvement in recommendation accuracy (Johnston 2012).

Mechanism: Bringing the winning improvement into production required substantial engineering effort just as Netflix’s business was shifting from mailed DVDs to streaming. That shift also produced richer viewing signals and changed what personalization needed to optimize.

Impact: The additional accuracy did not justify the production effort, so Netflix did not deploy the Grand Prize code.

Fix: Netflix retained two algorithms from the earlier Progress Prize and redirected its recommendation work toward streaming-era personalization.

Systems lesson: An offline metric gain is only valuable when its integration cost and objective still match the production system.

Johnston, Casey. 2012. “Netflix Never Used Its $1 Million Algorithm Due to Engineering Costs.” Ars Technica, April.

Figure 17 formalizes this selection pipeline as a multi-stage triage tree. Candidate architectures route first by input data structure (text, sequences, spatial grids, or tabular features) and then pass through sequential screening gates for memory capacity, compute budget, inference latency, accuracy targets, and hardware deployment readiness. High-cardinality recommendation workloads sit outside this dense flowchart and route to the sparse embedding family (section 6.8) before applying the same memory, compute, speed, accuracy, and deployment checks. If any constraint fails, the decision loop routes execution back to scale the model down, increase its capacity when accuracy falls short, or substitute an alternative architecture family.

The framework executes in three ordered phases, with model compression as the systematic way to scale a candidate down when it fails a budget:

  1. Data symmetry identification (\(\text{D}\)): Identify structural symmetries and invariances in the input data. Spatial locality routes candidates to CNNs; temporal sequence structure to RNNs; unconstrained cross-token dependency to transformers; tabular features without spatial geometry to MLPs; and high-cardinality categorical IDs to sparse embedding tables.
  2. Progressive physical constraint filtering (\(\text{A} \times \text{M}\)): Filter candidate models against hardware budgets: static parameter storage in flash/DRAM, peak dynamic activation footprint in SRAM/HBM, arithmetic intensity against the hardware roofline, and tail latency deadlines. A violation requires scaling down model width or depth, applying quantization and compression, or switching to a lower-overhead architecture family.
  3. Empirical capacity and deployment validation: Verify that candidate capacity meets target accuracy on representative evaluation distributions. If predictive accuracy falls short, scale model capacity and re-evaluate physical hardware constraints. If target accelerator hardware cannot sustain the required arithmetic throughput or memory bandwidth, select an alternative architecture rather than relying on marginal hyperparameter adjustments.

Kernel-level choices refine this decision. Im2col or direct convolution, fused normalization, and IO-aware tiled attention (FlashAttention: IO-aware attention optimization) change realized traffic and utilization, and can shift which iron-law term binds; ML Frameworks and Hardware Acceleration develop them. Architectural dependencies still matter. Batching independent recurrent sequences or fusing their operations reduces per-step cost, while the ordered state transitions remain. Candidate selection and kernel measurements therefore inform each other.

6.9.5 Architecture selection in practice

Every selection must first pass a feasibility check: whether the target hardware can sustain the required frame rate at all. A server-class vision pipeline on a mid-range GPU shows the throughput ceiling calculation in its simplest form. The wildlife monitoring deployment that follows applies the same checks under a watt-scale budget where GPUs are ruled out, which is where data structure, model capacity, and hardware budgets interact.

Napkin Math 6.3: The throughput ceiling
Problem: A real-time application must process 30 FPS of video with ResNet-50. What sustained compute throughput is required?

Math:

  1. Model cost: ResNet-50 requires ~8.2 GFLOP per \(224{\times}224\) image.
  2. Frame rate: 30 FPS required.
  3. Sustained throughput: 30 FPS \(\times\) 8.2 GFLOP = 246 GFLOP/s.
  4. Effective throughput: 10 TFLOP/s peak \(\times\) 50–60 percent utilization = 5 TFLOP/s–6 TFLOP/s.
  5. ResNet-50 headroom: 5 TFLOP/s \(\div\) 246 GFLOP/s = 20.3×.
  6. Detection-model check: 30 FPS \(\times\) 100 GFLOP = 3 TFLOP/s, leaving 1.7× headroom at the lower effective-throughput estimate.

Systems insight: A mid-range GPU delivering 10 TFLOP/s theoretical peak achieves 50–60 percent utilization in this planning scenario, yielding 5 TFLOP/s–6 TFLOP/s effective. For ResNet-50 at 30 FPS, the system has 20.3× headroom. Switching to an object detection model at 100 GFLOP per frame, however, requires 3 TFLOP/s sustained, leaving only 1.7× headroom. Batch size constraints or multi-stream processing quickly push the system toward the compute ceiling. The headroom assumes the GPU stays busy; a single 30 FPS stream runs at batch one, where kernel launches and small matrices usually hold utilization well below this planning figure.

The throughput ceiling converts an abstract compute requirement into a concrete hardware utilization percentage. A real-world deployment adds physical constraints the ceiling alone does not capture.

Worked example: Real-time wildlife monitoring

The design task is an embedded ML system that classifies wildlife species from camera trap images in an isolated national park. The deployment must execute inference entirely on-device with zero network connectivity, operate on solar-recharged battery power for six months, and achieve 90 percent accuracy across 50 target animal species. The walk-through applies the three-phase framework in five steps. Data characterization is the symmetry phase. Deployment constraint profiling, candidate architecture evaluation, and hardware budget checks carry out the constraint-filtering phase. Operational risk assessment sets up the validation phase that only target-device measurement can complete.

The first step characterizes the data. The input consists of camera trap image frames (\(1920{\times}1080\) raw sensor resolution, downsampled to \(224{\times}224\) for inference). Three properties favor a CNN candidate: spatial locality (fur textures or ear shapes occupy bounded neighborhoods), label invariance to translation (moving an animal within the frame should preserve its species label), and hierarchical composition (edges combine into textures, anatomical parts, and organisms). Convolution provides equivariant intermediate features that follow the animal’s location; pooling can then support an invariant classification. Local weight sharing supplies a compact feature detector for these images.

The second step, constraint profiling, translates the deployment environment into hard hardware boundaries. Table 12 catalogs these physical constraints alongside the architectural requirements each imposes:

With physical constraints established, the third step evaluates candidate architectures against reference model baselines. The planning scenario reserves 100 MB of the available RAM for model weights, leaving the rest for activations, system software, and runtime buffers:

  • ResNet-50 (25.6M parameters, 8.2 GFLOP): A costly starting point for the 2 W envelope. Its weights fit once quantized (25.6 MB in INT8 against 102.2 MB in FP32), but its arithmetic does not: at the platform’s 2 GOPS of sustained INT8 throughput assumed below, one frame takes about 4.1 seconds against a 500 ms target.
  • MobileNetV1 (4.2M parameters, 1138 MFLOP): A viable footprint and operation count. Its low arithmetic intensity, which can leave a data center GPU’s matrix units idle, costs far less on a batch-one edge CPU whose much smaller compute peak is easier to keep busy. Storing weights in INT8 reduces model footprint to 4.2 MB (16.8 MB in FP32), and factorized depthwise separable convolutions reduce arithmetic operations.
  • KWS DS-CNN (200K parameters, 20 MFLOP): An unmatched baseline. This model targets audio keyword spotting, so its reported size and task results do not establish accuracy on visual species classification. An adapted compact vision model would require training and evaluation on the camera-trap task.

While MobileNetV1 demonstrates the viability of depthwise separable convolutions, MobileNetV2 offers inverted residual blocks with linear bottlenecks as another compact candidate. Sizing the model with width multiplier 0.75× yields ~2.6M parameters (10.4 MB FP32, 2.6 MB with INT8 weights) and costs ~418 MFLOP at \(224{\times}224\) input resolution. Its weight footprint fits the allocation; task evaluation must establish whether its capacity meets the accuracy target for the 50 species.

The fourth step, hardware sizing, checks the candidate against physical platform budgets. INT8 weights occupy 2.6 MB, the network’s largest activation tensor, a \(112{\times}112{\times}72\) expansion inside its second bottleneck block, occupies 0.9 MB in INT8, and the scenario reserves 50 MB for OS and runtime buffers. Their 53.5 MB subtotal is only a single-tensor screen. Peak memory must instead include the weights, the maximum sum of simultaneously live tensor bytes, kernel workspace, and OS and runtime buffers. Inputs and outputs can coexist, and a residual path retains its input while the branch executes. The scenario has not yet established that live-tensor peak or the required workspace, so the small subtotal supports candidacy without confirming a fit in 512 MB of RAM.

For the candidate system-on-chip (SoC), an ARM Cortex-A53 at 1.2 GHz with NEON SIMD, this planning calculation assumes sustained throughput of ~2 GOPS INT8: \(\frac{418 \times 10^6 \text{ INT8 ops}}{0.002 \times 10^{12} \text{ INT8 ops/s}} = 209 ms \text{ latency} \ll 500 ms \text{ target}~\checkmark\)

Active power represents the final physical gate. At an estimated active inference draw of ~200 mW over 209 ms, each classification consumes 41.8 mJ. For a trigger-based rate of 100 inferences/day, model execution consumes 4.2 J/day. Full-system energy must also include image capture, sleep current, storage, and peripherals. Battery capacity, conversion losses, trigger frequency, and seasonal solar yield determine whether that daily budget supports six months of operation.

The fifth step assesses operational risks in the field. Table 13 pairs the primary accuracy, thermal, and coverage failure modes with corresponding systems engineering mitigations:

The sizing exercise selects MobileNetV2 (0.75× width multiplier) quantized to INT8 as a candidate for an ARM Cortex-A53 SoC with 512 MB RAM. Under the assumed sustained throughput, the arithmetic estimate is approximately 209 ms per frame. Qualification now requires target-device measurements of end-to-end latency, peak memory, thermal behavior, and full-system energy, together with classification accuracy on representative camera distributions and seasonal battery tests. In production, this qualification phase regularly exposes counterintuitive trade-offs: models with fewer FLOPs can stall on bandwidth-limited hardware, expressive architectures can degrade on datasets lacking matched structure, and lab prototypes can fail against deployment memory hierarchies.

Self-Check: Question

  1. The workload profile gives the transformer two rows, prefill and decode, even though both run the same weights. Under the table’s representative reuse and streaming assumptions, which statement correctly explains why the binding iron-law term can differ between them at small batch size?

    1. Prefill binds on the data term because it must read the whole prompt from storage, while decode binds on the compute term because each token requires a full forward pass.
    2. Prefill processes all \(S\) prompt tokens together, which can raise effective reuse and arithmetic intensity enough for the compute term to bind under the stated assumptions; decode processes one token at a time with reread weights and a growing KV cache, so the data term often binds at small batch.
    3. Both phases bind on memory capacity, because the KV cache always exceeds device memory.
    4. Decode is compute bound because its \(\mathcal{O}(S^2)\) attention scores dominate, while prefill is latency bound because tokens are processed serially.

  2. The chapter notes that the intensity column of the workload profile doubles as an energy column. Which statement correctly explains why a batch-one MLP or transformer decode step spends far more energy per useful operation than a convolutional layer, even on the same accelerator?

    1. The MLP and decode step perform more multiply-accumulates per weight than a convolution, so their arithmetic energy is higher.
    2. The CNN’s spatial locality lets it skip most of its multiply-accumulates, so it performs less arithmetic overall.
    3. All three load weights from DRAM once per batch, so their energy per operation is the same; only latency differs.
    4. The MLP and decode step load each weight from DRAM and use it about once per request, so DRAM traffic dominates their energy, while a convolution reuses each filter load across every position of its feature map and amortizes that movement.

  3. In the systematic Architecture Selection Decision Framework, if a candidate model fails the inference speed or memory budget constraint check on the target device, the engineer must immediately abandon on-device edge execution and route all inference to a cloud data center.

  4. In the wildlife monitoring edge deployment case study (50 species classification on a 2 W battery-powered Cortex-A53 device with 512 MB RAM and a <500 ms latency target), explain why MobileNetV2 (0.75 width multiplier with INT8 quantization) was selected as the candidate to validate over ResNet-50 and KWS DS-CNN.

  5. A dense MLP layer running batch-1 FP32 inference reports an arithmetic intensity of \(\approx 0.5\text{ FLOP/byte}\), while an image convolution bottleneck layer achieves \(>50\text{ FLOP/byte}\) on the same accelerator. Using the roofline model and an accelerator ridge point of \(150\text{ FLOP/byte}\), explain what follows from these numbers and what additional convolution measurement is needed before calling the convolution compute bound. Also explain why upgrading to an accelerator with double the peak TFLOP/s but unchanged memory bandwidth will not speed up the batch-1 MLP.

  6. A production profiler reveals that a model’s embedding tables consume over 1 TB of memory across cluster nodes, inference requests perform sparse random row lookups rather than dense matrix multiplies, and accelerator compute units remain over 95% idle. Which lighthouse archetype best represents this workload’s dominant system constraint?

    1. DLRM, because the binding constraint is memory capacity for terabyte-scale embedding tables accessed via sparse, irregular memory gathers.
    2. ResNet-50, because it stresses dense matrix floating-point throughput across regular convolutional grids.
    3. GPT-2, because autoregressive decoding is the canonical memory-bandwidth-limited serving workload.
    4. MobileNetV2, because depthwise-separable convolutions produce low arithmetic intensity on server GPUs.
